<meta>This is the first page of the translation. The text appears to be a transcript from a Rosicrucian manuscript (denoted by 'R. C.') discussing the "First Matter" and the astrological/alchemical generation of metals within the earth.</meta>

# From a certain Manuscript of a Rosicrucian Philosopher
## Excerpt.

<margin>Concerning the <term>Philosophic Mercury</term><note>A symbolic "liquid" or spirit sought by alchemists, not common quicksilver.</note> and the menstrual Matter<note>original: "Maa [Materia] menstruali." In alchemy, a 'menstruum' is a solvent that can dissolve or 'animate' a solid body.</note>.</margin>
Whenever the **Sun** and **Moon** <note>representing the gold/male and silver/female principles</note> wrap themselves together in the final rays of **Aries, Taurus, and Gemini**, this mixture of celestial influences is named by the Philosophers as their "First Matter" or **Mercury**. If these influences are captured and condensed in the proper vessel in the required manner, and then poured over "Spanish virgin mercury," you will be able to extract a floating oil within a 48-hour period. This oil is separated from the "black Saturnine Earth" <note>The dark, impure residue left over after heating, associated with the planet Saturn and the color black.</note> by filtration. This oil is called by the philosophers their "Venus," the "Green Lion," and the "Moon." If it is joined in this way with gold that has been artfully and correctly prepared, it will quickly be dissolved into running quicksilver, just as ice melts in lukewarm water. This substance is the matter closest to gold—that "one thing" which all Philosophers seek and find, but which common chemists have never seen. — H.E.

<margin>The generation of metals. See the work on the Three Principles, book no. 9, volume 8, page 8.</margin>
This covering (namely, of the earth), while it still remains porous, allows pure quicksilver—which has been separated from water and earth through <term>sublimation</term><note>The process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it.</note>—to enter into pores that are also pure and not at all defiled by waste-like earth or water. Because the substance is fixed there like a hermetically sealed glass vial, it becomes **Gold** (☉) through proper cooking <note>the metaphorical 'digestion' of the material via steady heat</note>. If the cooking is insufficient, it becomes **Silver** (☽). If the womb <note>original: "matrix," referring to the place in the earth where metals "grow."</note> is defiled by the numbing vapors of the stars, it becomes **Lead** (♄). If there is a mixture of numbing and life-giving influences, it becomes **Tin** (♃). If it consists of destructive, burning influences, it becomes **Iron** (♂). If it is a mixture of burning and numbing influences, it becomes **Copper** (♀). If it is a mixture of life-giving and burning influences, it becomes **Antimony** (♁)—or rather, it is the burning part of Antimony that causes the imperfection of the metals.

<summary>This page describes the preparation of a "Philosophic Mercury" or "Green Lion" through astrological timing and the condensation of celestial influences. It also provides an alchemical theory on how different metals are generated within the earth based on the "purity of the pores" and the balance of celestial heat.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucian, First Matter, Mercury of the Philosophers, Green Lion, generation of metals, astrological influences, sublimation, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the generation of metals from the previous page, specifically focusing on how the "generative part" of a substance can transform imperfect metals into gold and act as a medical cordial. It transitions into a cosmological explanation of planetary influences and the formation of celestial bodies.</meta>

# ON THE GENERATION OF METALS

...it burns away [the dross] of metals, but with the perfect generative part, it tints and improves. By this same reasoning, it works similar effects in animals, purging harmful humors <note>In ancient medicine, humors were the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health.</note> and strengthening the rest. But if its burning parts are separated and the generative parts are preserved, it becomes a supreme cordial <note>A medicine intended to invigorate the heart and spirit.</note> without any other addition. But just as the former imperfect metals are changed into gold (☉), when a harvest <note>original: "mess" (messis), referring to a gathered essence</note> of these generative parts is introduced in the right proportion, it is able to weigh down and subdue the remaining parts and reduce them to a similar holiness. This is just as experienced commanders change the disposition of an army from a five-fold or square formation to any other shape in an instant by the addition or subtraction of soldiers at the base, etc.

Gold (☉), since it is the most excellent of all earthly things, consists of one part light, three parts water, three parts mercury (☿), and one part earth, from which a perfect harmony results.

Electrum <note>A naturally occurring alloy of gold and silver, highly prized in alchemy.</note> consists of a terrestrial base of six, but possesses a celestial harmony; it is forged from nine parts Light, 2 of water, 3 of mercury (☿), and 1 of earth. These compose a double octave or a triple fifth <note>The author is using musical intervals to describe the mathematical ratios of the elements.</note>. He who has ears to hear will necessarily pay attention to this celestial music.

If the aforementioned gold is first fermented with quicksilver <note>original: "arg. vivo" (argentum vivum)</note> along with its specific properties and generative figures until they obtain the dominion of a right-angled triangle, and then—by some specific body, either celestial or terrestrial, according to whatever course is eminent in the whole universe and in each individual—it particularly ferments that which is multiplicative and projective of its species, whether it be star, metal, mineral, plant, or animal...

<column-break/>

# HARMONY OF THE PLANETS

...it may be animated; nor is there anything imperfect, deviant, monstrous, or sterile in the whole of nature, if the method of generation is perfectly understood. These methods vary with the species of almost the entire world.

Here we must observe the great and ineffable Wisdom and kindness of God, who out of the seven planets established only one as absolutely destructive due to its extreme penetration—namely Saturn (♄)—and one also absolutely destructive due to its numbness and inability to act—namely Mars (♂)—and four amicable ones: the Sun (☉), Jupiter (♃), Venus (♀), and the Moon (☽); so that the number of friends doubles the number of enemies. He left one, Mercury (☿), as indifferent <note>Meaning neutral or adaptable.</note>, composed from the extreme and middle geometric proportions of the rest; and thus the most wise Moderator <note>God</note> instituted and ordained it in the Zodiac and among the fixed stars.

The seeds of things, thus formed in the abyss by the meeting of stellar rays, are tossed here and there, and through perpetual contact, they grow as they move along, like a snowball, until at last they are powerfully attracted by a magnet composed of similar parts as they approach, and so they become cohabitants of the same body. But if they are tossed in the abyss for a long time, they pile up into a huge mass, grow, and thus become either a star or a comet, which differ only in their duration.

-> Folio 34. <-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the generation of metals and the nature of the "Stone" from the previous discourse on planetary harmony. While the previous page focused on the proportions of light, water, and earth, this page clarifies the true nature of the "Stone" against literalist misconceptions and introduces a new section on Hermetic treatises.</meta>

From the Golden Key of the Gate by G. R. <note>original: "Ex Clave Aurea Portæ G. R." "G.R." likely refers to an author or initiate associated with the "Golden Key" tradition of alchemy.</note>

**Our Stone**, presents itself basely in a form which is in every thing and in every place. What does this mean? For many, understanding this literally, take for themselves various and especially foul substances <note>Alchemists often used "filth" as a metaphor for the "prima materia" (first matter), leading many misguided "puffers" to literally experiment with waste products.</note>, which they distill and mix with much sweat and labor. But let them hear the words of the Philosophers:

> If you seek the secrets of the Philosophers in filth,
> You waste your effort because there is only deception at the end of your labors.

There is another point, almost like a delusion, that drives those occupied in this art: namely, that **Our Stone** is born between two little mountains, cast out into the dung-heap, trodden upon, and defiled by the feet of men. It is held as the most vile and contemptible thing, within itself and among us, etc. **Our Stone** cannot exist in things of a different kind from its own, namely, those differing from the nature of the <term>Sun (Gold) ☉</term> and the <term>Moon (Silver) ☽</term>. For nothing gives what it does not possess. A nettle does not sprout roses. It is established that nothing in this world, whether animal, vegetable, or mineral, can be generated without natural heat and a specific appetite. Therefore, according to the doctrine of the Philosophers, which explains everything through examples, it must be understood: The **Stone** can be in every thing and everywhere, since it is nothing other than a specific virtue and quality, provided that the natural heat has brought the composite matter to its perfect and necessary Limit.

For the **Stone** is the purest matter, containing within itself the vegetable heat of the nature of the <term>Sun (Gold) ☉</term>, from which it has the power of multiplying seeds in its specific and natural form. Because of this, it is called the Secret of Nature's <term>Fire Δ</term>, exciting the composite and perfecting it in our glass vessel into the stone, in exactly the same way that a seed—by reason of its own natural heat and radical moisture <term>Water 🜄</term>—leads to putrefaction, and then to a wondrous generation and multiplication.

<meta>This page transitions from the general philosophical discussion of the "Stone" into the specific manuscript titled *Speculum Luminum* (Mirror of Lights). The owner of this volume notes its provenance, stating it was acquired in London in 1649 during the aftermath of the English Civil War. The text on Folio 1 begins with a traditional religious invocation, common in alchemical texts, positioning the "Art" as a gift from God intended for the "poor seekers."</meta>

# This Chemical Manuscript
## with the Title,
# Mirror of Lights.
-> perhaps acquired by me in London at <-
-> Westminster Hall, in the year 1649. <-

Among other writings of a certain Irish Judge, <unclear>seized</unclear> by the fortune of the Civil War and sold off by himself. <note>The English Civil War (1642–1651) led to the displacement of many officials; Westminster Hall was a center of both law and commerce where books and manuscripts were often sold.</note>

---

<page-num>Fol 1.</page-num>
<header>Mirror of Lights</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

"Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, from the Father of Lights" <gloss>James 1:17</gloss>. And that "all things were made through Him, and without Him was nothing made" <gloss>John 1:3</gloss>. Him, therefore, I humbly invoke and entreat that He might mercifully deign to give me the light of His divine wisdom, so that through my knowledge I might be able to show with clarity the lights that lie hidden in the darkness. 

For I, the least of all <term>Alchemists</term> <note>original: "Alkymistarū"</note>, have compiled—not from my own wisdom but from the grace of the most high God—one ineffable art of alchemy. I have collected this little work from the best of all tested and perfected works of the leading philosophers, not for my own praise or glory, but for the benefit of the poor seekers and the perfected. 

Hence, I have titled this book by the name **Mirror of Lights** <note>original: "Speculum luminum"</note> because of the truth within the <term>alchemical operations</term> <note>The word "opibus" refers to the practical works or "labor" of the laboratory.</note>; it leads in this book from the darkness of errors to the light of splendid truth, for if one walks in the right lights, it directs them so they do not deviate from the instructions into error. Therefore, for whoever wishes to proceed through the doctrine of this book and to work, it will lead them through straight paths and be present in their labor. For in this book, a light so delightful and lovely is given that [otherwise] it lies hidden in darkness.

<summary>This page contains the title and provenance of the "Mirror of Lights" manuscript, followed by a devotional preface that attributes alchemical success to divine grace rather than human pride.</summary>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>Continuing from Folio 1, where the author introduced the work as a divine gift for "poor seekers," this page provides the structural outline and the theoretical foundation of the manuscript. The text transitions from the general title to the specific philosophical reasoning behind the "Mirror of Lights," identifying the primary components of metals (Sulfur and Quicksilver) and the ultimate goal of Nature: the production of Gold.</meta>

# Mirror of Lights.

It consists of this, because in this book he shows how the **luminous stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, often described as a source of light or a transformative "projection" powder.</note> miraculously illuminates dark bodies and so forth. Because in this book he teaches how to perfectly make the **two great lights** <note>original: "duo luminaria magna"</note>, namely, the greater light of the Sun <note>Gold</note> and the lesser light of the Moon <note>Silver</note>. This book is rightly named the **Mirror of Lights** because he divided this "mirror" into three parts:

In the **first part**, alchemy is said to be diligently investigated.
In the **second part**, the conditions pertaining to alchemy are more frequently noted.
In the **third part**, the true and perfect works of alchemy are more clearly declared.

Every building requires and demands a firm foundation. Since they wish the work of the <term>transmutation</term> <note>The changing of one metal into another, usually base metals like lead into gold.</note> of metals through alchemy to be set upon a solid and well-grounded base, it is expedient for investigators to see if this science is firm and true. Therefore, in this first part, the form proceeds as follows: First, the reasons of the philosophers are premised; second, authorities and objections are brought forward; and third, the solutions to doubts are placed, from which both the firmness and the truth of this art become clear.

Wherefore, regarding this science, all the writers of alchemy—namely **Aristotle, Avicenna, Anaxagoras, Hermes, Alquinus, Dansin, Rusinus, Morienus, Geber, Rhazes, and Albertus** <note>A list of the "Authorities" of medieval alchemy, blending Greek, Arabic, and Scholastic thinkers.</note>—are of one mind and in agreement. However, they first proposed certain reasons, the first of which is this: Every metal is generated and composed from **sulfur and quicksilver** <note>original: "sulphure et argento vivo"</note>, just as the matter of herbs is generated from the earth. So it is 

<column-break/>

that the aforesaid philosophers and experts agree: every mixture, generation, and corruption arises from those principles into which it can be resolved. All metallic things are of this sort; for all metals are generated and composed of three parts. But experience sufficiently teaches that all metals can be most immediately resolved into sulfur and <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury. In alchemy, "Sulfur" and "Quicksilver" often refer to philosophical principles (Soul and Spirit) rather than the raw chemical substances.</note>, just as a metal is generated and composed from the same. 

And Quicksilver is the source and origin of all metals through itself; in the principal region it is dominant in the metal in a greater excess, acting as the source and origin of any quicksilver of this kind. Because in noble quicksilver, sulfur exceeds in the metal; sulfur provides the hardness, that is, the excessive hardness of metals is from the region of sulfur. However, without it, no metal would be malleable or liquefiable; to those states, the quicksilver overcomes the sulfur itself. Because it is through the earth, due to the destruction of the quicksilver, that the species is changed; and the earth is joined with the quicksilver, and by a very temperate <term>decoction</term> <note>A slow heating or "cooking" process intended to mature the substance.</note> over a long time, it is hardened and becomes the mined stone from which the metal is extracted.

In the beginning of the generation of metals, **Nature** always intends to produce pure gold through quicksilver by itself. Nature always intends to produce the perfect, and produces the species accordingly. But since the two aforesaid metals <note>Gold and Silver</note> are perfect, nature—when not hindered—always intends them; however, according to the aforementioned philosophers, this intention of nature can be diverted or altered by other metals.

<summary>This page defines the three-part structure of the *Mirror of Lights* and establishes the "Sulfur-Mercury" theory, which claims all metals are composed of these two principles. It asserts that Nature’s ultimate goal is always to create gold, though this process is often interrupted.</summary>

<keywords>Mirror of Lights, Sulfur, Quicksilver, Mercury, Transmutation, Alchemical Authorities, Generation of Metals, Gold, Silver, Nature's intention</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the Mirror of Lights and established the metaphysical foundation of the "stone" and the "two lights" (Sun and Moon). This page moves into the practical and theoretical alchemy of metal formation, specifically the Sulfur-Mercury theory, explaining how various metals like gold, silver, and lead are composed of the same basic ingredients in different states of purity.</meta>

### Speculum Luminum

...[they sig]nify <unclear>that</unclear> if Sulfur and foul earth are present, it produces a stain in the metal. If indeed the occasion arose from corrupted matter, appearance, and moisture, <margin>Note</margin> it results in a failure. When red and clean Sulfur is mixed with <term>quicksilver<note>original: "argento vivo"</note></term> in clean earth, the nature of gold is created through constant decoction <note>The process of "cooking" or heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or mature it.</note>. If, however, the sulfur is white, clean, and mixed, silver is made. But if red sulfur, corrupted by burning <note>original: "adustionis"</note>, is mixed in a [certain] <unclear>quantity</unclear> of [corrupted earth], copper will be made.

But if white sulfur, corrupted by burning, is mixed in a clean place, tin will be made. Whence it flows according to its <unclear>hardness</unclear> and speed because of a defect in the mixing of quicksilver with sulfur. When indeed corrupted white sulfur is mixed with quicksilver in clean earth, iron will be made. But from black and corrupted sulfur mixed with the [weight] <unclear>lead</unclear> of quicksilver in clean earth, lead will be made; and it is said that the silver which is lead is "fallen gold" <note>This refers to the alchemical belief that base metals are simply "diseased" or "imperfect" versions of gold.</note>. And they say that metals differ among themselves only by <term>accidental forms<note>original: "formas accidentales" – in Aristotelian philosophy, these are non-essential qualities like color, smell, or hardness that do not change the underlying "essence" of a thing.</note></term>. 

Likewise, what is <unclear>judged</unclear> by the natural philosophers regarding metal, they say is determined only by colors, purities, and corruptions, and thus these are accidental forms; they say there are only accidental forms in metals before he introduces the seals of the metals. <unclear>Likewise</unclear>, quicksilver does not differ from gold in itself except in this: that the sulfur in gold is red, while the sulfur in quicksilver is white, because [it lacks] <unclear>coldness</unclear> and <unclear>perfection</unclear>. 

All metals are generated from sulfur and quicksilver, but whiteness and redness are accidental forms—namely qualities—and they say pure quicksilver and pure gold possess only accidental forms. And likewise, copper does not differ from gold in this: that the sulfur 

---

in gold is clean, and gold is generated in clean earth, while [in copper] there is foul sulfur in which there is corruption; and copper is generated in unclean and foul earth from which corruption <unclear>follows</unclear>. But purity and corruption are accidental forms—that is, qualities—and they say pure gold and copper possess only accidental forms. And likewise, copper does not differ from quicksilver itself except in this: that the sulfur in quicksilver is itself clean and white, and pure quicksilver is generated in clean earth. But through the sulfur in copper, it is red and corrupted, and copper is generated in foul and unclean earth from which it produces corruption. 

But whiteness and redness, purity and corruption are accidental forms—that is, qualities—and they say pure quicksilver and copper possess only accidental forms. And likewise, tin does not differ from gold except in this: that the sulfur in gold is red, clean, and well-mixed. The sulfur in tin is white, corrupted, and not well-mixed. 

But whiteness, redness, impurity, and corruption are accidental forms; they say pure gold and tin possess only accidental forms. And likewise, tin does not differ from quicksilver itself except in this: that the sulfur in quicksilver is clean and well-mixed with the quicksilver. But purity, corruption, and mixture are accidental forms; they say pure quicksilver and tin possess only accidental forms. 

And likewise, iron does not differ from gold itself except in this: that the sulfur in gold is red and clean, and gold is generated in clean earth. [Whereas] the sulfur in iron is white and corrupted, and iron is born in [foul] earth...

<summary>This page outlines the Sulfur-Mercury theory of metallogenesis, arguing that all metals are essentially the same substance but differ in their "accidental forms" such as color, purity, and the cleanliness of the earth where they formed.</summary>
<keywords>Sulfur-Mercury theory, quicksilver, gold, silver, copper, tin, iron, lead, accidental forms, decoction, alchemical generation</keywords>

# Mirror of Lights.

### Page 7

...infected and blackened by hope, and having found the waters infected and turbulent, they were thus desolated and exhausted, and despaired of the whole art.

¶ The 6th vision is that some performed good and pure <term>sublimations</term><note>Sublimation: The process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then collecting the purified solid that forms upon cooling.</note>, but when testing the powder, it was clearly of no value. Wherefore they adulterated copper by adding <term>haematite</term><note>original: "haematitem"; a reddish-black iron oxide mineral.</note> and <term>aetites</term><note>original: "etites"; also known as "eagle-stones," these are hollow geodes believed to have magical or medicinal properties.</note> in place of silver; by whitening the copper in this way, they deceived themselves and others.

¶ The 7th vision is that some sublimated "spirits" <note>In alchemy, "spirits" usually refer to volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic.</note> fully and <term>fixed</term><note>Fixation: The process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or escapes when heated.</note> them, along with whatever else they did, but the substance would not penetrate the metals and would not tint them.

¶ The 8th vision is that some treated spirits that were fully sublimated and fixed with a penetrating oil; it had the power of <term>ingression</term><note>Ingression: The ability of an alchemical elixir to "enter" and permeate a base metal to transform its inner nature.</note> into metals. They added gold in place of silver, and thus tinted copper into an "alchemical metal" <note>original: "alchymiam"; here referring to a simulated or synthetic precious metal.</note> which was as cheap as silver in its whiteness and remained malleable up to the 3rd <term>examination</term><note>Examen: A trial or assay, usually involving intense heat, to test the purity of a metal.</note>, but after the 3rd examination, the white color vanished and it became red copper.

¶ Notwithstanding these objections, this science which is called <term>alchemy</term> is true and is the highest art, provided that those who are perfected in it work through the "true roots." 

1. First, by fully sublimating and fixing the spirits. 
2. Then, preparing solutions, fermentations, and distillations from the powders. 
3. After this, coagulating the distilled waters. 
4. Next, whitening the <term>calxes</term><note>Calx: The powdery residue (oxide) left after a metal or mineral has been roasted or "calcined."</note> and the calxes of metals for the work, and reddening the red ones. 
5. Afterwards, reducing the whitened and reddened calxes back into masses of metal. 
6. Finally, in a red mass with red...

It concludes by defending alchemy as a true science when practiced through a specific sequence of sublimation, fixation, and calcination.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Mirror of Lights," a treatise exploring the defense of alchemy against skeptics and the technical requirements for successful transmutation. The previous page listed "visions" or common errors; this page addresses specific objections to the art's validity.</meta>

# Mirror of Lights.

<margin>rather</margin> than <term>coagulated</term><note>Coagulation: the process of turning a liquid or vaporous substance into a solid.</note> tinctures which lose their specific nature on their own, they work according to the science. They produce gold and silver better than all natural gold in its malleability and blending, because alchemy is a most true and most firm science; thus the objections are answered.

¶ To the first objection, it is said that Silver speaks and says of those who believe and wish to transmute metals that are neither <term>calcined</term><note>Calcination: the process of heating a metal to high temperatures until it oxidizes and turns into a powder (calx).</note> nor prepared, that it can undoubtedly hardly be done; as Silver says: "I do not believe that metals can be <term>sublimated</term><note>Sublimation: in alchemy, heating a substance to turn it into vapor and then collecting the purified solid that forms upon cooling.</note> unless they are reduced to their primary matter, namely into <term>calces</term><note>original: "calces"; the powdery residue or "ash" left after calcination.</note> through the burning of fire and cleansed of their corruption." And this is what <term>Hermes</term><note>Hermes Trismegistus: the legendary Egyptian sage considered the founder of alchemy.</note> says: "Species cannot enter bodies unless they are prepared."

¶ To the 2nd objection, I say that this does not prove a defect of the science, but rather a defect of the vessels and a defect of the person who directed the manner of working.

¶ To the 3rd objection, it is said that it is no wonder if the species evaporated, because they were not fully prepared; they were not <term>fixed</term><note>Fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when heated.</note>, not dissolved, not <term>fermented</term><note>Fermentation: adding a "ferment" (like gold or silver) to a prepared substance to give it the properties of that metal.</note>, nor distilled, nor coagulated. And therefore, when projected upon molten metals, they were not able to endure the fire; they departed, and consequently, the metals could not be tinted.

¶ To the 4th objection, it is similarly said that the objection proves a defect in those who did not wish to wait for the end <note>A common alchemical trope: the failure of the work is blamed on the practitioner's impatience.</note>, and not a defect in the science.

¶ To the 5th objection, it is said that the reason why the species was infected and blackened was due to boiling <unclear>is</unclear>, and it was because those desiring to know the skill of the art did not wait in the proper manner.

<column-break/>

# Mirror of Lights.

<meta>Catchword at bottom of previous page: "it wears/rubs" (terit).</meta>
rather it is named after gold for such a color, because white is stronger than silver <gloss>tincture</gloss>. Rather it is named after gold for such a destruction, the same substance remaining and not being destroyed. There is much matter through which much is done more often. For <term>sharp water</term><note>original: "aqua acuta"; a corrosive acid or solvent used to dissolve metals.</note> can often be made, by which any free bodies can be transmuted from color to color; this is clear enough in dyed things, where tinctures and paintings are used.

¶ One might say perhaps that this is true for those colors which are not in things by nature but come from the outside; hence such colors are "colors within color," as in painted things. But in colors that are in things by nature, it is not true, since such colors are inseparable "souls." <gloss>others</gloss> Truly it is otherwise in silver, for it is white by nature, not through destruction, while the <unclear>gold</unclear> itself still remains. For white comes from <term>arsenic</term><note>Arsenic was frequently used in "whitening" copper to produce a silver-like alloy.</note> by nature and from natural arsenic, and red [likewise]. And then we see that silver, through decoction and persistence, is black, and through sublimation it becomes white.

¶ And so we also see through art it is naturally black because the earth is black, but through calcination it is always red. Thus it is that colors are naturally within things. If, however, they cannot be destroyed while the things remain undestroyed—as Silver claims, saying that it more often destroys the form of the species—one might again say that such species can easily be changed from color to color, but this takes time in metals. As we see, silver was a white color, that is, like the "flower of silver."

¶ Likewise, we see that copper takes on color again...

It also explores the nature of color in metals, distinguishing between external "paints" and the "inseparable souls" of natural color.</summary>

# Mirror of Lights.

From calamine stone <note>original: "lapis calaminari"; a zinc ore used in making brass</note> with <unclear>mll</unclear> <note>possibly "mel" (honey) or "materia" (matter)</note> it is perfected; namely copper, for it is like calamine stone because fire acts upon both. ¶ Likewise, we see that from tin, litharge <note>original: "litargirum"; usually a lead oxide, here suggesting a byproduct of refining</note> is made, and from tin through excessive burning <term>adustio</term> <note>a chemical "scorching" or oxidation</note> it takes on the color of gold; for this reason, a change into sulfur or with sulfur in tin and elsewhere is possible. ¶ Likewise, we see that from lead, ceruse <note>original: "cerusa"; white lead used as a pigment</note> is made, and from ceruse, minium <note>original: "minium"; red lead</note> is made, and from minium, lead is made. This is either because metals can be transmuted from color to color, or because of this whiteness <term>albedo</term>, just as [one moves] from gold by a defect of substance, though the substance itself is not defective. ¶ Otherwise, the alteration from white to red is gold, if it is proven by the aforesaid philosophers <note>original: "physicis"; referring to natural philosophers or alchemists</note>. ¶ Every red metal that is clean and not corrupted is gold, just as an alteration from white to red is metal proven clean and not corrupted, because it was never corrupted. If the alteration from white to red is gold, it can be made gold through the arts in another way if not by itself; for however it is changed from white to red, gold can be made. If it happens by itself, then it is through the arts; or if it is changed from white to red by these means, then by the skill of the art gold can be made. ¶ Every corrupted metal can be prepared through the arts to be free from its corruption, because according to all natural philosophers and physicians, medicine can be made for all infirmities; therefore every corruption of metals is an infirmity. Whence the philosophers call corrupted metals "sick" and "fallen" <note>original: "infirma et lapsa"</note>, as are all corruptions of metals.

<column-break/>

# Mirror of Lights.

Through the arts, a purgative medicine can be made, and through this every corrupted metal can be purged by the arts from its corruption. ¶ Therefore, metals are not corrupted naturally, but by accident, such as through the corruption of Sulfur and foul or unclean earth. Corruption is not inherent in the metals by a current defect—not a defect of the metal itself—but every corruption of metals can be purged through the arts. Whence Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century scholar and patron saint of scientists, widely cited in alchemical texts</note> says that as far as the metallic body is concerned, corruption is calcination <term>calcinatio</term> <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile impurities</note>, and from this are made ash and lime <term>calx</term>. This is ground and washed with sharp water <note>original: "aqua acuta"; usually a strong acid or vinegar</note> until it is white and clean. Then, through the lime and various medicines, it loses this corruption and burning, and receives a foreign rectification <note>a process of purification or "straightening"</note> into its body. When it is restored in the lime, it is reduced into a metallic mass, and once melted it receives further the healing medicine—that is, the white and red tincture <term>tinctura</term>. Then it is among the perfected metals, and the cleansing is joined, removing every corruption it had from sulfur and from the earth. ¶ Every perfected metal, purged of all sulfurous and earthly corruption, can through the arts become white or red as it pleases the craftsman, because every metal can be transmuted from color to color according to the will of the craftsman in this way. ¶ Every perfected metal, purged of all sulfurous and earthly corruption, can be transmuted from color to color according to the will of the craftsman, just as every perfected metal is purged.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of "curing" base metals like lead and tin of their "sickness" (impurities) to reach a perfected state. It emphasizes that corruption is accidental rather than inherent, allowing metals to be cleansed through calcination and "sharp water" before being tinted gold or silver.</summary>

...by art, [a substance can] be made white or red, according to the will of the craftsman, once it is freed from all sulphurous and terrestrial corruption. ¶ Every perfected metal that has been purged of sulphurous and terrestrial corruption and whitened <term>dealbatum</term> <note>the process of "whitening," a key stage in alchemy called albedo</note> is pure quicksilver <gloss>living silver</gloss>, whether that metal was previously copper, iron, tin, or lead. This is made clear by examining each of the aforementioned metals individually. For this "A" is a clean metal, lacking all corruption <gloss>no</gloss> <note>The gloss "ne" likely stands for "negatione" (by negation) or "non" (not)</note>, because by <unclear>possibility</unclear>, "A" is fully purged of all sulphurous <gloss>no</gloss> and terrestrial corruption. # Mirror of Lights.

...to be fully purified <note>original: "p<re>fari," likely "purificari"</note> from every corrupt color and whitened if by itself from those things which [result in] <gloss>cause 5</gloss>. It is the case that if by art, copper, iron, tin, and lead can become silver <gloss>cause 6</gloss>, because copper, iron, tin, and lead can by art be made into pure gold <gloss>cause 7</gloss>. For whatever copper, iron, tin, and lead can be fully purified from all corruption and rust, they can become pure gold. If <gloss>cause 8</gloss> by themselves from the six [metals], whichever they are, copper, iron, tin, and lead can be fully purified by art from their corruption and rust, if they possess it by themselves. <gloss>cause 9</gloss> whichever and that if by art copper, iron, tin, and lead can become pure gold. From the aforementioned things it is evidently clear that the science which is called <term>alkyia (alchemy)</term> is a true science and a firm art. For alchemy is the preparation of metals; wherefore alchemy is said to be from "alkimi" in Greek <note>original: "alkimi grece"</note>, which is a "broad mass" <note>This is a common medieval folk etymology attempting to link the word to the physical mass of the metals</note>. For by this art the mass of metals, which in their mines are corrupted and imperfect, are brought back from corruption to health and from imperfection to perfection. <gloss>cause 10</gloss>

<margin>Solution</margin>
But some object <gloss>cause 11</gloss> against the science through the word of Aristotle, who says that if the craftsman of alchemy knows [them], the <term>spirits (volatile substances like mercury or sulfur)</term> of mixed metals are not able [to be fixed]. And that many have found many good things in the operations of alchemy and yet achieved nothing because through the first [way] they have <gloss>cause 1</gloss> a face [appearance] of the science that is neglected <note>original: "obliatur," likely "oblivio" or "oblitio"</note> because of porous vessels the spirit would exhale. <gloss>cause 2</gloss> and it was seen that some neglected the spirits to the full, yet even if the vessels are fine and not porous, and besides with those spirits they were not able to perform [the work], because they did not act well upon the molten metal. <gloss>cause 3</gloss> and <gloss>cause 4</gloss> it was seen that some performed neglects, solutions, and decantations...

# Mirror of Lights.

Wherefore they tinted copper that was not calcined, not cleansed of its corruption, and not whitened to the white state as the science teaches. Aristotle then shows a personal defect in that he was ignorant of the science, and it is not a defect of the science itself. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmutation of metals, specifically transitioning from the corruption of lead to the "conditions" (requirements) of the alchemical work and the properties of Quicksilver.</meta>

# Mirror of Lights.

There are many things pertaining to alchemy <gloss>original: alkymiam</gloss>. For some are <term>conditions</term> <note>Requirements or necessary states of matter.</note> which according to some are perpetual, and likewise some are instrumental. These secondary <term>conditions</term> are all those conditions which belong to the essence of the whole internal work. If the opposing parts are complete, they belong to the essence of the whole.

<margin>Material conditions</margin>
And the <term>conditions</term> are many, for there are material conditions and certain formal ones. The material conditions are all those things from which the internal work is made, such as metallic bodies, spirits, stones, flowers, limes <note>original: calces; chemically altered powders produced by heating</note>, ashes, powder, glass, iron dross <note>original: samma feri / scoria ferri</note>, wine vinegar, fresh water, human urine, and the urine of children, concerning all of which we must treat in order. Truly, the metallic bodies are gold, silver <note>original: azentum, a variant of argentum</note>, copper, iron, tin, and lead, which are called by the philosophers by the names of the planets. For the philosophers named gold the Sun; silver, the Moon; copper, Venus; iron, Mars; tin, Jupiter; and lead, Saturn. Of these bodies, some are perfect and some are imperfect. The perfect bodies are the Sun and the Moon. The imperfect bodies are Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn. If you look at the imperfect parts, the imperfect bodies require for the work of alchemy that they be fermented with a strong spirit, and by these means the spirit dyes the bodies and transforms them into another [state], both red and white. Whence the perfect bodies, namely the Sun and Moon, are called the soul <note>original: anima/aia</note>, because from them and from the spirits the stone is made, changing dead bodies and transforming them into Sun and Moon.

<column-break/>

And therefore Hermes says: "The stone," he says, "is not found except in an animated thing." If the imperfect bodies are required for alchemy, it is because through the artifice called alchemy they are led from corruption to health. If you look at the perfect parts, they are brought back.

<margin>Properties of the highest alchemy</margin>
If any flower should come from the imperfect bodies, the works are: quicksilver <note>original: azentum vivum / argentum vivum</note>, sulfur, orpiment <note>original: anzipimentum / auripigmentum</note>, and arsenic <note>original: azesum</note>. If it should be in the spirit, it is prepared so that it does not flee the fire; they make [gold and silver] from mercury and from ions, Moon from Venus and Saturn. If you wish me to speak briefly: through these, being fermented with the souls, all metals are transmuted into Sun and Moon, better than natural gold in all operations, as the spirit is able to endure through alchemy. They are not produced from adamant, and the gold of alchemy does not [corrupt] the human heart; it is not animated by the stone, and from it wounds are healed, which is not the case with natural gold. In all bird-like <note>potentially "higher" or "volatile"</note> operations, the soul remains in the metals and the spirit and in the colors until the end.

<margin>Properties of quicksilver</margin>
And finally, the spirit, namely quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, is a viscous water in the bowels of the earth, a stable white earth of a very clear color. It is a unique union... it is flat and does not easily adhere except by its own aquatic nature, although it has a viscous substance because of its purity, which obstructs its own path and does not permit it to adhere. And it is the mother of metals along with sulfur, namely the red stone of which it was said above. Quicksilver is found in the mountains and especially in closed veins <note>original: cloacf vētibz / clausis venis</note>. Its nature is cold and moist, and from it all metals are produced.

<summary>This page defines the "material conditions" of alchemy, listing various ingredients like urine and vinegar before detailing the planetary associations of the seven metals and the viscous, "mother" nature of mercury.</summary>

## Page 10

...the same, and without it no metal can be gilded; it is the source and origin of all metals, for it has the planets [the metals] as its own. <margin>Note on Sulfur</margin> Quicksilver is the reason why. If indeed there are <term>alembroz</term> <note>Likely sal ammoniac or a specific alchemical salt mixture.</note>, <term>belzi</term>, and <term>antaxit</term> <note>Obscure alchemical terms for mineral substances, likely of Arabic origin.</note>.

¶ The **second species** is **Sulfur**, which is the "fatness of the earth" in the mine, thickened by a tempered <term>decoction</term> <note>The process of heating or boiling a substance in a liquid to extract its essence.</note> until it hardens and becomes thick; and when it has been hardened, it is called Sulfur. ¶ Sulfur is of the strongest opacity, for it has a uniform substance in its parts, and therefore the oil is not taken from it [easily] as it is from other things that contain oil. Rather, through "sharp water" <note>Probably a mineral acid or strong vinegar.</note>, sulfur is sent into the earth during these decoctions—some immobile, some in <term>palumbibus</term> <note>Possibly "lumps" or a specific vessel shape.</note>. And it is of many types: namely, white, red, yellow, black, green, living, and dead. It is "living" if it comes directly from the earth and is found pure and not melted, and [if] you keep it in a vessel. It is "dead" if it is that which has been melted in <margin>Note on Orpiment</margin> <term>reeds</term> <note>Latin: canis/cannis; referring to the tubes or molds used for casting sulfur sticks.</note> as it is sold in the apothecary shop.

¶ The **third species** is **Orpiment** <note>Latin: Auripigmentum; a golden-yellow arsenic mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy.</note>, which is a mineral stone; and thus the "regimen" is made from those burnt in earthen tubes, and through long decoction, it passes into the substance of orpiment. There is a double viscosity in it: one is subtle, the other is thick. One is removed by washing and decoction, <margin>Note on Arsenic</margin> but the other is removed by urine, which is taken away through <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance so it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used to purify "spirits."</note>.

¶ **Arsenic** is of a more subtle matter, having a sulfurous color, and it is a red stone. Its nature is indeed like that of orpiment. And indeed, there is one viscosity that is removed by washing and decoction in urine, which is removed by sublimation. Indeed, its "flower" is white and red, and it is easily sublimated.

<column-break/>

# Mirror of Lights.

<margin>What Salt is</margin>
It is whitened in three ways: namely, by sublimation and decoction. ¶ The **Salts** are **Sal Ammoniac**, **Common Salt**, **Alkali Salt**, and **Azure Salt**. These are metallic, being both natural and artificial. The natural ones are those found in the earth, namely white and red, whose virtue consists of a hard mineral vapor without violent falsity. Their nature is indeed hot and dry, and it is healing, cleansing, and elevating.

Artificial salts are those made by art, and they are used in many ways in the work of alchemy. Natural salt itself is more noble than all other salts and is volatile before the heat of bodies and spirits in water. For with it, they "roast" and dry substances out, and by placing it in some liquid to dissolve, it is dissolved in water with the iron and many other things; or the silver itself is an oil which is a coagulated dryness. Its nature is hot and moist, a species of subtlety for the **elixir**, for without it the elixir is neither dissolved nor does it "enter" [the metal]. And you should know that the "heart" [of the metal] is neither whitened nor reddened nor transmuted by the salt itself, but through the "species" <note>The specific alchemical quality or form being introduced.</note>. Rather, the salt itself gives entry to the spirits and bodies and purges the heart of blackness, and then it leaves the species mixed, united, and reddened with the bodies.

¶ **Common Salt** is the **key of this entire art**, because it opens and closes all things, and without it, no work of alchemy can be performed. But **Alkali Salt** pertains to the art in such a way that when the salt is well prepared, it reduces all the heats of the bodies of the work into a solid mass, for its nature is indeed hot and moist.

<summary>This page defines the primary "spirits" of alchemy—Sulfur, Orpiment, and Arsenic—describing their physical origins and purification through sublimation. It further categorizes various Salts, identifying Common Salt as the "key" to the alchemical art and Sal Ammoniac as the essential agent for allowing the elixir to penetrate metallic bodies.</summary>

<margin>Note: What the stones are</margin>
The stones are: Human hair. Blood. The hen's egg.
<margin>stones</margin>
These are called the "stones of the philosophers." Hair is indeed called the "red stone" found in various places, which is born on two mountains <note>original: "nascitur in duobus montibus"; this is a common alchemical allegory for the dual nature of the material or specific anatomical sources.</note>. Blood is indeed the hidden, precious stone, because it is hidden within the human vein; it is precious because it is of a friendly nature <note>Meaning it is compatible with the human constitution or the "life force" of the work.</note>. The hen's egg is indeed the noble white stone. These stones are used for the art of <term>alchemy</term> <note>Latin: "alkā", a common abbreviation for "alkamia".</note> because from them are produced waters and oils which stabilize all spirits.
<margin>Note: What the flowers are</margin>
Blood assists in the making of <term>sal ammoniac</term>. ¶ The flowers <note>In alchemy, "flowers" usually refer to the fine powder or sublimate collected after heating a substance.</note> are: flower of tartar, flower of alum, flower of antimony, flower of copper <note>Latin: "eris", from "aes".</note>, flower of <term>ernabi</term> <note>Likely a corruption of an Arabic term like "arnab" (lead) or an obscure mineral.</note>, flower of minium [red lead], and flower of vitriol. From the flower of tartar an oil is made in which all <term>calxes</term> <note>The powdery oxides left after a metal is burned.</note> of bodies and spirits are dissolved into water; and it is one of the "fixing agents" <note>Latin: "retinaculis", literally "tethers" or "retains". These substances prevent volatile spirits from evaporating.</note> of spirits, and with it, the spirits are fixed. ¶ Flower of alum is mixed in <term>sublimations</term> <note>The process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then a solid again.</note> and from it a water is made for all alchemical reductions. ¶ Flower of antimony is produced from antimony, which is an earth with no odor, having sulfur by means of calx; and from this flower a red water is made with which spirits and bodies are reddened with a permanent red color which they will never lose. ¶ Flower of copper is above all things for the art, for from it indeed is made a red water by which spirits and bodies are reddened with a permanent red color which it never loses; and it is one of the "fixing agents" of spirits and

<column-break/>

fixes all spirits; in this water are many secrets. This flower is indeed called <term>atincar</term> <note>A term derived from Arabic for borax, used as a flux in soldering and chemistry.</note>. ¶ Flower of *ernabi* is a noble thing which pertains to the art, and is also called *unifaz*. From this flower a red water is produced with which spirits and bodies are reddened with a permanent red color that never fails. And from the flower of minium and the flower of vitriol, red waters are made with which bodies and spirits are turned red with a permanent red color which they never lose.

sal ammoniac and salt alkali. ¶ The ashes are: ashes of wine dregs [tartar], ashes of glass, ashes of human blood, and <term>clavellated</term> ashes <note>Latin: "clauslati"; essentially potash or "ashes of the keys," produced by burning the tartar found in wine casks.</note>; namely, ashes of clean things. Through them, white tinctures come to satisfy the dyeing of cloths. These ashes are found instead of salts; they make a powder that pertains to the sublimation of mercury. Iron scale <note>The flaky dross that falls from iron when it is forged.</note> pertains to the sublimations of <term>orpiment</term> <note>Reading the OCR "anzipicti" as "auripigmenti".</note> and arsenic, and they are also sublimated to iron soot. Wine vinegar is mixed in many decoctions, in the preparations of spirits, in sublimations, in the <term>calcining</term> <note>Reducing a metal to a powder through heat.</note> of the Sun [gold] and the Moon [silver], in the preparations of flowers, and in the preparation of sal ammoniac. Fresh water pertains to the preparation of common salt and salt alkali, and for washings. Human urine is prepared for the sublimation and decoction of orpiment and arsenic. The urine of boys is for the making of sal ammoniac and for the solutions of the stone.

# Mirror of Lights.

...things. Regarding the "bad conditions" <note>In this context, "bad" or "evil" likely refers to common, impure, or preliminary states of matter rather than moral evil.</note> from which the alchemical work is made, this intended purpose is sufficient to be noted. And not yet, since many things pertaining to alchemy are from these "bad" sources, namely: Saltpeter, Rock Salt, Salt of Vetch, Borax, Calamine stone, *torpet* stone <note>original: "lapis torpet." This may refer to a "numbing" stone or a specific mineral like a variety of magnetite or even a corrupted spelling of topaz.</note>, Tutty stone <note>Zinc oxide collected from furnace flues.</note>, Hematite stone, Cadmia, Marcasite, Magnesia, Litharge <note>Lead oxide.</note>, Saffron of Iron <note>Iron oxide.</note>, Orpiment <note>Arsenic trisulfide.</note>, and many other things which are not held as necessary in the works of this book; and therefore, concerning these <margin>No strong conditions</margin> things, they shall not be treated in this "mirror." 

¶ The **Strong Conditions** are the operations directed toward the end of our present intent, which are: Preparations, Sublimations, Fixations, Calcinations, Solutions, Fermentations, Distillations, <margin>preparation</margin> Coagulations, and so forth. 

¶ **Preparation** of medicinal things is, namely, their due arrangement. Prepared are: Salt ammoniac <term>Sal armoniacum; ammonium chloride used as a flux</term>, Common salt, Alkali salt, "flowers," water of flowers, oil and water of tartar, and the stones of the philosophers. 

¶ <margin>Sublimation</margin> **Sublimation** is the elevation of a dry thing by fire, together with its adherence to the vessel <note>The process of turning a solid into vapor and back into a solid on the upper parts of a flask.</note>. Sublimation is indeed diversified by the diversity of the "spirits" to be calcined; for some sublimation is done with a moderate fire and some with a strong fire. If it is the sublimation of mercury, orpiment, or arsenic, some sublimation is done at a low fire. If it is the sublimation of Sulfur, it must be so because it will not whiten <note>meaning it may burn or discolor if the heat is too high</note>. Mercury is sublimated so that its "earthiness" may be set aside and its "wateriness" removed. Whence, in sublimations, the earthiness of mercury is cleansed by the lime of white marble,

<column-break/>

# Mirror of Lights.

...from the powder of the finest glass, in a threefold way: through prepared Salt ammoniac and through prepared Common salt. The method for the removal of its wateriness is this: when it is mixed with those things with which it ought to be sublimated, let it be ground and mixed until it no longer appears <note>meaning the mercury is "killed" or thoroughly incorporated into the base</note>, and let it be agitated over a gentle fire until the receding water is removed; and let it be agitated <margin>fixation</margin> until the mercury recedes a second time. 

¶ **Fixation** is the adaptation of a melting substance to withstand the fire. The reason why fixation is performed is so that every "tincture" <note>the coloring principle or essence</note> and every alteration may persevere in the other substance and not be changed. For the "mystic bodies" <note>likely referring to the metals or the nascent philosopher's stone</note> are fixed through their calcination, since they are dissolved from their volatile "Sulfur-nature." 

Species are fixed in this work in six ways: 
1. By frequent reiterations of Sublimation until they remain in the vessel; if one speaks of Sulfur and Arsenic, they can then be sublimated so that they will remain and be "fixed." 
2. In the second way, species are fixed with oil of tartar. 
3. In the third way, with metallic waters. 
4. In the fourth way, by themselves. 
5. In the fifth way, with the waters of the philosopher's stone. 
6. In the sixth way, with their own oils. 

<margin>Calcination</margin> ¶ **Calcination** is the reduction of a thing to powder through the deprivation of the moisture of its consolidating parts <note>Reducing a substance to ash or oxide by heat.</note>. Bodies are calcined from their diminished perfection; diverse species are also calcined. Bodies are calcined so that the "Sulfur-nature" existing in them and corrupting them may be destroyed. For it burns away all "Sulfur-nature" and whatever is a scorched thing, since without calcination it could not be destroyed. Soft bodies are calcined so that they may be hardened by the calcination; for they can be hardened from a manifest force and are hardened more quickly. Species are calcined so that they may be more...

# Mirror of Lights.
### Page 15

...purifies and dissolves more quickly in water, because every <gloss>earthly</gloss> lime is more fixed and easier to dissolve than that which has not been calcined <note>Calcination is the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or "lime."</note>. For this reason, it is said that spirits do not enter bodies unless they are purged and hard; hence, soft bodies are calcined. Soft bodies can be moderately heated and easily calcined by the fire <unclear>of water baths</unclear>. Hard bodies can be calcined by a fierce fire.

<margin>Solution</margin>
Solution is the creation of a liquid from any calcined thing. For there are salts, flowers, and stones of bodies, so that their intensity may become <unclear>extended</unclear> <note>original: "extensum", here meaning spread thin or thinned out into a liquid state</note> and thus they are ready for distillation. Solution is brought about by heat and moisture, or by cold and moisture.

<margin>Fermentation</margin>
Fermentation is the proper and proportionate mixing and joining <gloss>of such a quantity</gloss> of souls and spirits. Just as bread is leavened by a good ferment, so the masses of metals are transubstantiated into gold and ruby through a medicine made from spirits and animals. Wherefore, in the true medicine of the elixir, the name "elixir"—which is both Arabic and Latin—is interpreted as "ferment." <note>The "ruby" refers to the Red Work, the final successful stage of the alchemical process.</note>

<margin>Distillation</margin>
Distillation is the raising of watery vapors within their vessel. Distillation is divided because some is done without fire and some with fire. Distillation performed with fire is twofold: one is done by raising (as in water made by an <term>alembic</term> <note>An alembic is a classic alchemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</note>), and the other is by descent (as in water made through a pipe). Indeed, distillation by a pipe is the purification of liquids from their dregs. When the spirit is pure water, it is the "imbibition" <note>The process of a solid absorbing a liquid.</note> of spirits and pure medicines, so that the water contains no dregs, for purified spirits and medicines could otherwise be

<column-break/>

infected. That distillation by descent through a pipe is the <unclear>extension</unclear> of pure oil in its own nature.

<margin>Coagulation</margin>
Coagulation is the reduction of the liquids of a thing to a solid mass through the evacuation of moisture. This process signifies a measured hardening and the release of dissolved medicines from their mixed wateriness. They coagulate in fire by the frequent precipitation of violence upon them through the harshness of the fire; for the harshness of the fire removes their wateriness.

<margin>Waxing</margin>
<margin>Ceratio</margin> <note>Ceratio or "waxing" is the process of softening a hard substance until it has a wax-like consistency.</note>
This is done in a long and narrow vessel. Waxing is the softening of non-flowing and non-fluid things, both moist and dry, into a liquid state; this is achieved with fixed oils of sulfur and arsenic, with oil of tartar, and similar known substances.

<margin>Medicinal Precepts</margin>
Just as in the <gloss>books</gloss> of the learned <gloss>philosophers</gloss> pertaining to others. These instructional books contain the rules that must be observed in this art.

<margin>1</margin>
The first is that the craftsman should be cautious and keep these things secret; he should keep his counsel to himself, because if his work were made public, it would be considered a forgery, and he would be left in danger with an unfinished work.

<margin>2</margin>
The second precept is that the craftsman of this art should have a secret place away from the sight of men, in which there are two rooms—specifically one in which the operator stays to fuse, calcine, dissolve, and distill.

<margin>3</margin>
The third precept is that he should observe the proper time in which the work is to be done; for fusing, solutions, and distillations are not effective in the winter time. Fusing and calcining, however, can be done at any time.

<margin>4</margin>
The fourth precept is that the craftsman of this art...

<meta>This page continues the technical and ethical instructions for the alchemist. The previous page discussed the chemical processes of calcination, solution, and fermentation. # Mirror of Lights.

He must be diligent and frequent in his work, and not lazy, but should persevere until the end; for if he begins and does not persevere, the matter is then lost. And if he works for seven days and remains idle for the next seven, and thus delays from year to year, he will never achieve perfection.

<margin>5th Precept</margin>
¶ The 5th precept is that he should work according to the art, <insert>namely</insert> according to custom: that is, first in solutions; 2nd in sublimations; 3rd in fixations; 4th in fixations <note>The original text repeats "fixation" twice, likely emphasizing the difficulty of making a substance stable under heat</note>; 5th in solutions; 6th in fermentations; 7th in distillations; 8th in coagulations. This must be done in order, because if he performs the earlier steps after the later ones, the <term>sublimated</term> <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a substance into vapor so it collects as a purified solid</note> substances—not being <term>fixed</term> <note>Fixation is the process of making a volatile substance remain stable and non-evaporating when exposed to fire</note>—will not remain, but will immediately fly away. And if he performs the earlier steps with fixed powders, he will not be able to dissolve, ferment, or distill them; nothing will appear because they will not enter the "bodies" <note>Metals were often referred to as "bodies" in alchemical theory</note> nor will they tint them.

<margin>6th Precept</margin>
¶ The 6th <insert>third</insert> precept is that all vessels in which the dissolved medicine is to be placed and prepared for the sun must be made of glass or be glazed. For if <term>sharp water</term> <note>original: "aqua acuta," referring to a corrosive acid or solvent</note> is placed in a copper vessel, the water will turn green. If placed in vessels of iron or lead, it will be blackened and ruined. If in an earthenware vessel, the water will soak through the vessel and be lost along with the liquid.

<margin>7th Precept</margin>
¶ The 7th precept is that the practitioner of this art should not involve himself in any operations for princes and rulers because of two evils. For if he involves himself with such people, they will inquire from time to time and say to him, "What is being done? When shall we see something good?" And because they are unable to wait

<column-break/>

# Mirror of Lights.
## Page 12

for the end of the work, they will say the time is up even if it is not, and he will face the greatest shame. Furthermore, if he does not reach a successful end, he will face their indignation forever. If, however, he does reach a good end, they will imprison him forever so that he cannot leave, but must remain <note>The author warns that a successful alchemist becomes a "golden prisoner" to a greedy king</note>. ¶ The 8th precept is that no one should involve themselves in works that are not fully explained for at least two years, so that he may learn to perform all things necessary to the art. For if he involves himself and works, and in these specific tasks he <insert>fails</insert>, then the matter is lost. This is not to be understood only as regarding the person's work, but also if it happens that he errs in the operation and prolongs it, because by the interruption of the <unclear>art</ununclear> it may not meet the <unclear>necessity</unclear> before the end.

<margin>Books and instruments</margin>
¶ 9th: Knowledge of books for the sake of the <insert>law</insert> to others who belong. ¶ 10th: Instrumental books refer to all the instruments that belong to this art.

<margin>Concerning fire</margin>
Fire is said to be made from charcoal of wood. This is specified because it is less labor to use charcoal than to use wood in the furnace. Secondly, because fire from wood produces much smoke, and because of that smoke, one cannot see when the vessels crack from the heat. This often happens when the clay is not good or when the vessels are not well-baked; when they crack, a white smoke immediately exits, which is easily seen over a charcoal fire. When the vessels smoke, they must be removed from the fire immediately; otherwise, the <term>sublimation</term> <note>the purified material collecting at the top of the vessel</note> contained within them will be lost.

<summary>This page outlines the strict order of alchemical operations, the necessity of using glass vessels to avoid contamination, and a stern warning against working for powerful patrons who might imprison a successful alchemist. It also provides practical advice on using charcoal instead of wood to better monitor the integrity of the glass vessels during heating.</summary>

# Mirror of Lights.

Oh, caution! And let there be caution in these operations. For some vessels are wooden, and some are of lead or copper; some are of unglazed earth, and some are glazed, all of which will be shown later in the instructions for the operations.

<margin>Method for furnaces</margin>
¶ There are many types of furnaces: some are the "philosophical furnace," some are for <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of turning a solid into vapor to purify it</note>, some for <term>distillation</term> <note>Heating a liquid to create vapor that is then condensed back into liquid</note>, and some for glazing. The philosophical furnace should be built near a wall where the wind cannot strike it, or it should stand away from the wall at the distance of an arm's length, in this manner: Let a pit be made in the earth to a depth the size of two <term>spans</term> <note>A span (spanna) is an ancient unit of length, roughly 9 inches or the width of a human hand with fingers spread</note>, which is lined with "master's clay" <note>original: "argilla magistri"</note>. With this clay, build a wall in a circle. Take simple clay and add to four parts one part of good potter’s clay, mixing it very well, and add a little sand; with this mixture, build the wall. The pit should be two feet high and one span in thickness; let it dry. Then, you must have a <term>grate</term> <note>original: "rota" (literally "wheel"), here referring to a perforated circular tray that holds the fuel or the vessel</note> made of good potter's clay that can withstand a strong fire. It should be perforated everywhere with small holes so that [ash] can easily fall down below. In the earth, let a passage be made between the pit and the wall, one span wide, before the grate is placed on top; and let there be an arch where the pit is open toward the wall, about one span wide, and the passage must be lined with clay which is placed below, from which the expansion

<column-break/>

# Mirror of Lights.

of the holes should be. Then, let a wall be built above the grate to the height of an arm and a thickness of a span, in the manner of a kiln. It should have an opening like a kiln's mouth. Let there be a lid [or cover] fitted to the top of the wall that has, through its thickness, four or six small holes the width of fingers; these are the vents of the furnace. ¶ Furthermore, it should be known that upon the grate one must place a tripod made of good potter's clay, upon which the <term>calcining vessels</term> <note>Vessels used for calcination, the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter</note> should be placed, with charcoal underneath. ¶

<margin>Method for a sublimation furnace</margin>
The furnace for sublimation and the furnace for distillation are made in this way: Take the same clay as described for the philosophical furnace. Let a round wall be built upon the ground to a thickness of four fingers, a width of three palms, and a height of two spans. Let the grates and passages be made in all respects as described for the philosophical furnace. The difference, however, is this: the sublimation furnace and the distillation furnace are open at the top, and upon these openings the vessels should be placed on tripods suited for them, so that it can be observed whether the vapors are breathing in or out. And it should be noted that the distillation furnace ought to be wider at the top than at the bottom, and the sublimation furnace ought to be wider at the bottom than at the top. ¶ The glassmaking furnace should be made in a spacious place between walls; let stones be placed in the manner of walls and build the wall of them with clay that well

A furnace is to be built from specific clay, with a foundation set approximately one span (about 9 inches or 23 cm) deep. A mound of the same clay should be built up around the base, extending about one span outwards. The furnace itself should be oblong in shape and have a cover. There should be small, straight openings, about a finger's width apart, arranged according to the furnace's design. Above these openings, vessels for calcination (the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air or oxygen) should be placed, utilizing heat from a cold chamber with coals. This setup suggests a controlled heating process for chemical or metallurgical applications.

**Transcript:**

De praeparatione / Sal communi

Multi furni fiunt; alii sunt physici, alii furni pro perstillando, alii pro vitrificando. Furnus physicus est sui generis, proprie factus pro partibus et pro sustinendis vasis furni, ut distet a basi sua ad longitudinem brachii. In hunc modum fiat fundamentum, foris, ex quadam portione ipsius luti, quod contineat massam. Desuper, **dasadzra imfzig**. Sed extra, fiat spatium inter fundamentum et basim, unius palmi. Et desuper, ponatur stratum, et fiat ex luto, et adhibeatur fundamentum circa basim, quasi ad unum palmum, et massa luti et luti quae superponitur et antea applicatur.

Pro foraminibus situs: tunc fiat tumulus supra stratum ad altitudinem brachii; est unus palmus in basi compacta, in modum furni. Et fiat oblongus in modum furni, et fiat operculum, et fiat brevis apertus tumulus recte. l. T. s. Foramina ponenda applicentur. Est digitalis latitudo secundum formam furni. Et desuper, vasa calcinationis e camera frigida cum carbonibus debent poni secundum stratum desuper. c.

**Translation:**

Of preparation / Common salt

Many furnaces are made; some are physical, others are for distilling, others for vitrifying. A physical furnace is of its own kind, properly made for its parts and for supporting the vessels of the furnace, such that it stands at a distance from its base of one arm's length. In this way let the foundation be made, on the outside, from a certain portion of the same clay, which contains a mass. Above that, **[German phrase meaning "that is fifty"]**. But outside, let there be a space between the foundation and the base of one span. And above, let a layer be placed, and let it be made of clay, and let the foundation be applied around the base, as it were to the extent of one span, and a mass of clay and mud which is placed over and applied beforehand.

For the placement of the openings: then let a mound be made above the layer to the height of an arm; it is one span in the compacted base, in the manner of a furnace. And let it be made oblong in the manner of a furnace, and let there be a cover, and let there be a short, open, straight mound. L. T. S. The openings to be placed should be applied. It is a finger's width according to the form of the furnace. And above, vessels for calcination from the cold chamber with coals ought to be placed according to the layer above. c.

# On the <term>spirit</term> of Sal Armoniac <note>In alchemy, a "spirit" refers to the volatile, vaporous essence of a substance. "Sal armoniac" is ammonium chloride, a vital salt used for cleaning metals and in various chemical reactions.</note>

<margin>
The spirit
of Sal
Armoniac
</margin>
You must distill that salt; specifically, the Sal Armoniac prepared for this purpose. 
**Take:** the old urine of boys, speak of it <note>original: *effabere* - likely meaning to describe or account for the amount</note>, and boil it. And while boiling, remove the scum <note>original: *nublam*, literally "the cloud"</note> that floats on top until it is pure and clean. Then remove it from the fire and let it stand in some vessel for the whole night. In the morning, strain it for the pure urine and separate it from the dregs, and throw away the dregs. Then, for every 8 parts of urine, add one part of <term>quicklime</term> <note>original: *calcis vive* - calcium oxide, a caustic alkaline substance</note> which has been ground; let it be mixed well with a rod <note>original: *bacto*, a shorthand for *baculo*</note> and let it sit for three days and nights. Every day in the morning, let it be stirred well with the rod so that the whole power of the lime descends into the urine. Then allow it to settle until the lime falls to the bottom of the vessel and the clear urine remains above the dregs. When it has stood clear above the dregs, draw it off, separating it from the dregs by straining through a thick, clean linen cloth and leather. 

Then take the <term>dregs of wine</term> <note>original: *feces vini* - often refers to tartar or potassium bitartrate found in wine barrels</note> and <term>calcine</term> them <note>Calcinating involves heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to oxidize it or turn it into ash.</note> well by themselves; [likewise] ashes of the vine, and calcine by themselves ashes of blood from a human vein. To do this: first dry the blood in the sun, and once dried, grind it into powder and put it in an earthen pot; close it and put it in the calcination furnace, burn it thoroughly, and calcine it into ashes. Then, from these ashes being very well ground, take an equal weight of each. Namely: **take** 3 pounds of the ashes of wine dregs, 8 pounds of the ashes of the vine, and 3 pounds of the "blood of glass" <note>original: *sanguine vitri* - this likely refers to a specific red chemical state or may be a scribal error for *sanguine vitis* (juice of the vine).</note> which has been well treated.

<column-break/>

Of the ashes of human blood, take 3 pounds. Let these be well [prepared], and divide the mixed ashes into 3 parts. Then take one aforesaid part and put it in ten times as much of the prepared urine. Mix it well and boil it well, so that the whole power of the ashes is extracted. When these are boiled, pour the whole into some vessel, straining it through a thick cloth suited for this purpose. Pour the strained water again, while hot, over the same dregs; heat the same water and pour it over the same dregs, straining it to make a <term>lye</term> <note>original: *lixivium* - an alkaline solution leached from ashes</note>. 

Then throw away those dregs and put the second part of the ashes into the same lye you made; mix it well, boil it, strain it, and do in all things as you did before for the second and third [parts]. Then throw away the dregs and take the third part of the ashes and put it in the same water with which you made the lye; do in all things just as you did with the first and second part. And then you will have a strong <term>capitellum</term> <note>original: *capitellum* - a technical term for a highly concentrated or "strong" lye used as a solvent in alchemy.</note>. 

Then take prepared common salt, and for 3 parts of that salt, add one part of strong vinegar and grind it well. Then dissolve that vinegared salt in ten times as much of the aforesaid hot, strong *capitellum*. Then let it be well mixed with a large spoon throughout this second step, so that the strong *capitellum* and the aforesaid vinegared salt are joined together. Allow it to infuse, and when it is finished, distill it through a filter prepared for this work. Then boil down the distillate in a cauldron until the water vanishes and it gives off no smoke; then...
