# On the preparation of Alkali Salt | On the preparation of Flowers

...allow it to cool <gloss>permittes infrigidari</gloss> and it is a hard stone which is called Sal ammoniac <term>Sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, a volatile salt used in metallurgy and alchemy</term>. <insert>To</insert> Place this salt in a new and dry pot in the furnace; this is done beforehand with it. If you have worked with common salt and its own [nature], since it has the property of salt.

<margin>Preparation of Alkali Salt</margin>
¶ Alkali salt is prepared thus. Take <note>original: *Recipe*</note> ashes of whatever plants in a large quantity of burned material, such as those which they use for dyeing cloths, and grind them very finely, and take a sixth part of common quicklime <term>quicklime: calcium oxide, a caustic substance produced by heating limestone</term>. Take: let it be strong, and let it be mixed, and place a thick cloth over a vessel suitable for this work, and place above it the ashes mixed with lime as much as the cloth can hold; then pour boiling water over it and strain it as lye <term>lye: a strongly alkaline solution reached by leaching ashes, used here to extract salts</term> until it exhausts all the bitterness, and then discard that layer and make another, and do to this as was done before; then permit the water to settle in that same vessel until morning, and then boil the salt in a cauldron until the water evaporates and does not give off smoke; then allow it to cool <gloss>permittes infrigidari</gloss> and it is a hard stone which is called alkali, and it is always of bitterness; then place this salt in a pot up to the middle and place the pot, unsealed, in the furnace and give it first <gloss>primo</gloss> a slow fire until it boils, afterwards give a better fire until it grows red-hot and melts. If it is removed, then pour it out immediately with iron tongs into another pot, because if it is delayed at all you cannot pour it out [since] it quickly hardens; then you will have Alkali salt

<column-break/>

white, which you place in a glazed vessel in a warm and dry place, because if it is placed in a moist place it will turn into water; and save it.

<margin>Preparation of flowers of tartar</margin>
¶ Flower of tartar <term>flower of tartar: a purified form of cream of tartar (potassium bitartrate)</term> is prepared thus. Take crude tartar of red and white wine and fill the pot with it and close it and put it in the furnace; give it first <gloss>primo</gloss> a slow fire and afterwards a strong one, and do this for two days and nights in which the pot grows somewhat red-hot; afterwards extract it and you have the flower of tartar, which you should save, for I will teach you how to make oil from it with which you can dissolve all the calces <term>calx: the powdery residue left after a metal or mineral has been calcined/burned</term> of bodies and spirits, and in that oil many secrets are contained.

<margin>Preparation of flowers of alum</margin>
¶ The ashy flower of precious stones should be prepared beforehand like the flower of tartar, from which are made waters and distilled oils through a tube <note>original: *per cannam*, likely referring to the delivery neck of a distillation apparatus</note>.
¶ Flower of alum is prepared thus. Take as much alum as you wish and put it in a pot up to the middle and, once closed, put it in the furnace and give it first a slow fire and afterwards a strong one until the pot grows red-hot; then allow it to stand for a day in that same fire; afterwards extract it when it has cooled and you will find an exceedingly white alum which is called flower of alum; save this flower because from it is made a water for all white varieties <note>likely referring to white metallic or alchemical preparations</note>.

<margin>Preparation of flowers of antimony</margin>

If it is cinnabar, when you see a mild smoke <note>original: "fumu[m]... bla[n]du[m]"</note> emerging, then it is sufficient. Then grind it into powder upon a stone without water, and you will have it prepared. ¶ Likewise, just as the flower of the moon <note>Silver (Luna) in alchemical terminology</note>—which is made from plates of silver suspended over the vapor of vinegar—is made, so too is the flower of copper <note>original: "flos eris"; likely verdigris</note> made. ¶ The flower of minium <note>Red lead</note> is prepared thus: let plates of lead be suspended over the vapor of strong vinegar in a strong jar; close the vessel and place it in a warm place. In all things, do with it as you would for copper green <note>Verdigris</note>, and you will find ceruse <note>White lead (lead carbonate)</note> adhering to the plates. Collect this and save it until you have a large quantity. Then calcine it <note>heat it to a high temperature without melting it</note> in all ways as you would for the green, and it will become a beautiful and white ceruse; and from that, make minium. ¶ Likewise, take the ceruse and grind it well upon a stone with red water, and make from it something like little cakes. Place these in an earthenware vessel that is not round, but long like the length of an arm. Afterwards, place a large pot upon three stones—over two supports <note>original: "pistos"; likely clay pedestals or bricks</note> made of clay, fashioned a palm's width apart. Upon those, place the pot again so that the mouth of the pot lies upon one support and the bottom upon the other. Then place the vessel with the ceruse in the middle of the pot; afterwards cover it

### **On the Sublimation of Mercury**

...sublimation, provide a gentle fire for half a day until all the moisture evaporates through the hole at the top of the **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic condenser pot used in the sublimation process</term>. You shall test this by placing a knife blade over the hole for a short time; if at that point the moisture has finished evaporating, no condensation will appear on the blade as it did before, for the moisture has then departed. Then, close the hole with **clay** <term>argilla: potter's clay used to seal alchemical vessels against air</term> and provide a stronger fire. In the final hour of the day, give a very strong fire, and when night falls, cease the heat and allow it to cool.

In the morning, open the vessel, and whatever has been sublimated will appear in the upper part of the aludel. The other part will sublimate like snow upon the surfaces and will turn to powder around the lower parts of the vessel. Collect that which is in the upper vessel and set it aside. However, if there was first a "cooking" <note>original: *coctura*; refers to a sustained heating process</note> with salt, sublimate it a second time; and finding it again white and powdery, continue the cooking of the whole and sublimate until you have it entirely **"dead"** <note>original: *mortuum*; in alchemy, a "dead" substance is one that has been "fixed" or chemically altered so that it no longer possesses its original metallic properties, such as the fluidity of quicksilver</note> and reduced to powder.

Then be careful: if the mercury does not seem to dissolve in water of **sal ammoniac** <term>sal armoniacum: ammonium chloride, a pungent salt used as a reagent to dissolve metals</term>, water of common salt, and **oil of tartar** <term>oleum tartari: a caustic solution of potassium carbonate</term>, it will immediately regain its liveliness <note>referring to its liquid, "quick" state</note>, and your labor would then be in vain. At that point, take common salt, prepared well as I have taught, and spread a layer of it. Add a portion of the sublimated mercury and mix it by hand so that it is free of all moisture. Place it in the **sublimatorium** <note>a vessel specifically designed for the sublimation of solids</note> and close it as before, sealing it with a feather <note>likely referring to the delicate application of a luting or sealing paste</note> in the manner previously described. Sublimate it in all respects as before. In the morning, you shall open it; collect that which has been sublimated and test if in the dregs...

<margin>Chapter 7. The first way of solution</margin> **F**ourthly, <term>solutions</term> <term>solution: the process of turning a solid substance into liquid form</term> must be shown. Whence it must be known that spirits are dissolved in three ways. In one way, if they are prepared with oil of tartar, namely by internal coagulation; it ought to be placed upon marble in a very damp cellar, and let the stone be inclined toward a glass vessel to receive the dissolved water.

### Chapter 51

Fifthly, the reddenings <note>original: "rubificationes," the process of turning a substance red, often a sign of increasing potency in the alchemical work</note> must be demonstrated, from which species are turned red: through red water, or the distilled flower of copper, or flower of cinnabar, or flower of red lead, or flower of vitriol. And with all this distilled water, you should soak the species which you wish to redden. 
By grinding, soaking, and roasting, then place it upon the stone to be dissolved, and it is dissolved into a very deep red water; and quickly the calcined bodies can be reddened.

### On distillation

<margin>Chapter 67</margin> Sixthly, the distillations must be demonstrated, where it must be known 
<margin>Method of distilling</margin> that if the distillation is made through water, put whatever you wish into the distillation vessel and place it in the ashes as was taught above. And distill it; this is done for the sake of charity <note>The author likely means "clarity" or "purity," or is using "charitas" in a metaphorical sense for the "kindness" of the heat</note>. If, however, you distill by means of heat and moisture, place the water in the vessel in which ashes were previously placed, or into a cauldron placed upon the distillation furnace. In that water, place the <unclear>narrow</unclear> vessel, and above it the glass, finally using ties so that it does not tilt to any side; it must be upright. You must take precaution never to place cold glass into hot water, as said above, otherwise the glass is destroyed and you will lose your labor. However, take care that the water does not boil during distillation; and by this teaching, distill all waters and so forth as above.

<margin>Method of distilling by descent</margin> All this is said according to the higher teaching and form. ¶ Distillation of water that is done by descent <term>distillation by descent: a method where the heat is applied to the top of a vessel, forcing the vapors or oils to move downward into a receiver</term> is from the highest clay vessel, fitted over the work and filled with ashes, or wood, or stones, or roots from which you wish to distill oil. Make a pit, and in it place a vessel carved out underneath, if it be so, and above it the vessel for descent. Close it well so that it does not breathe <note>i.e., so that the vapors do not escape</note> into the surroundings, and allow it to dry. Then stop up the vessel from above with earth. Afterwards, make a fire on the top only for one hour, increasing it no more than that in an hour, until half a day has passed.

# On Congealing

<margin>Chapter 7. 
For congealing</margin>
...smoothened and allow them to infuse; then open it and you will find water and a distilled oil, which you should reserve.
**S**eventhly, congealings <note>original: "conglacōes"; refers to the process of turning a liquid into a solid state, often through evaporation or cooling</note> must be made manifest. Regarding this, it should be known that the congealing of all "waters" <note>In alchemy, "waters" often refers to acidic distillates or dissolved metallic solutions</note> is produced by heat and dryness. Place whatever water you wish to congeal into a glass vessel having a narrow neck; place it in the ashes over a slow fire, and it will be congealed within [number unclear] hours into a white or red plate, and so on.

<margin>Chapter 8. 
For the calcination of metals</margin>
**O**ctavo, the calcinations <term>calcination|The process of heating a metal in air until it oxidizes and crumbles into a fine powder or "calx."</term> of metals must be made manifest. Regarding this, **Sol** <note>Gold</note> and **Luna** <note>Silver</note> are calcined thus: Take filings of whichever of these you wish and place them in strong vinegar for nine days. Once dried, grind them into a powder; afterwards, pour on "water of ammonia" <note>original: "aqua armōci"; likely sal ammoniac (ammonium chloride) dissolved in water or vinegar</note> while grinding and drying them. Then, place them upon a stone as I have taught you for the process of solution; distill and reserve. From this water, add to the waters of the powders; however, note that you must add the water of **Sol** to the red water, and the water of **Luna** to the white water.

**Mars** <note>Iron</note> and **Azarg** <note>original: "azarg"; likely referring to Mercury or a specific mineral like arsenic or lead depending on the local tradition</note> are calcined in this way: Take plates [of the metal] and heat them sufficiently. Afterward, smear them well with "water of common salt" <note>Brine</note>, because it corrodes all the corrupting moisture that is within the metallic bodies. Place them in a calcining vessel, fill the vessel with the plates, and close it. Place it in the calcining furnace upon a tripod; place coals beneath it and make a strong fire so that

<column-break/>

it does not melt <note>original: "no fluat"</note>, and in that heat allow them to stand until the moisture is destroyed by the fire. Always observe this: whatever is calcined, scrape it off and save it; whatever is not calcined, smear it again with the water of common salt. 

<margin>Another method of calcining</margin>
Do this until all is calcined. And if you prefer another way: take the plates and cut them to the width of a penny. Smear them with salt water and then wrap them well in prepared ammonia <note>original: "arfōco"; likely sal ammoniac</note> with vinegar. Then place them in a single layer, one layer over another <note>original: "stratū s̄z stratū"; the common alchemical technique of *stratum super stratum*</note> until the vessel is full. Then seal it with clay so that it cannot breathe <note>i.e., create an airtight seal</note> and allow it to dry. Then place it in the calcining furnace and let it stay there for nine hours, so that the entire vessel glows red-hot. However, do not make the fire too strong, lest the [metal] plates melt and liquefy due to excessive heat, which often happens. Afterward, allow it to cool; take it out and you will find everything destroyed <note>meaning the metallic structure is broken down</note> so that it can be easily ground in a mortar or on a stone. Grind it into a powder and reserve it. Smear the remaining parts that are not calcined again and calcine them as before, and so on.

<margin>Chapter 9. 
For the reduction of calx into a solid mass</margin>
**N**inthly, the reductions of calcinations must be made manifest. It should be known that the "calxes" <note>powders</note> of metals are reduced back into a solid mass. Take the calx of any metal you wish and grind it best upon a stone, and afterward wash it three times with hot water...

### On the reduction of lime into a solid mass | On stones

<margin>Chapter 19.</margin>

Then dry it very well, grind it <note>original: "tazere" – to grind or triturate on a stone</note> and add "white flower water" <note>likely a distilled floral water or a specific white chemical distillate</note> distilled and mixed with distilled common salt water. Grind it strongly and <term>imbibe</term> <note>to "imbibe" means to slowly add liquid to a dry powder so it is absorbed</note> with that water as many times as you grind, soak, and dry, until it becomes <term>quicklime</term> <note>original: "calx viva" – here meaning a chemically active metallic powder</note> and white, so that all sulfurous and corrosive corruptions are removed from it. When it has been fused, open the hole and pour the <term>medicine</term> <note>the alchemical substance in its active state</note> into an iron channel or into a stone mold, and you will see a white and bright mass. This mass can receive white and red tincture because it has been cleansed from all sulfurous and corrosive corruption. ¶ If, however, you wish to work toward a ruby [red stone], soak the lime of metals with "red water" made from the "flowers of the king," or <term>minium</term> <note>red lead</note>, or "fetid vitriol" <note>original: "atramento feto"</note>, and do everything as was said above at the end with alkali water. Pour it out as I taught and you will see a red mass. And thus, all things regarding the stones and their perfections are finally manifested.

<margin>Note well concerning stones</margin>
Take, in the name of the Lord: 1 part white mercury, 2 parts sulfur water, 3 parts arsenic. Mix all these together and add half a part of <term>Silver</term> <note>original: "lune" or Moon</note>. Place it in a glass vessel having a long and narrow neck; close the glass so that the seals are joined. Heat it with fire and with a red-hot iron for as long as it takes until the glass liquefies and the substance comes out. Then place it in ashes over the fire and congeal it. When it has been congealed, it is a firm <term>tincture</term> <note>a substance capable of "staining" or transforming other metals</note> that is penetrating, mixing, coagulating, joining, uniting, and preserving. Place one part of this tincture upon 100 parts of purified "iron gum" <note>possibly a resinous-looking iron slag or dross</note> or purified copper, and it is always good without doubt in every hammering and examination, and it will last forever. ¶ Likewise: take one part of <term>sublimated</term> and fixed mercury, another part of sublimated and fixed arsenic, and another part of the lime of silver. Make a stone well by soaking it with <term>sal ammoniac</term> water <note>original: "sal armoniacum" – ammonium chloride, used as a flux</note>. Do this three or four times by grinding and roasting as I taught above. Place the stone to dissolve; if it does not dissolve like a resin, and because it is not solvent, grind it thoroughly <note>original: "taze maliḡ" – possibly "maligne" (with difficulty) or a scribal error for "magis" (more)</note> and add a little sal ammoniac, and it will all be dissolved. When all has been dissolved, put it to distill as I taught in warm water, and take good care that you do not put it in ashes, because it would harden for the most part and it would be necessary to dissolve it again.

# The Mirror of Lights.

If first, when everything that has been distilled is placed in a glass flask <note>original: *cucurbita*, a gourd-shaped vessel used in distillation</note> and coagulated just as I have taught, and it is a white substance as transparent as crystal, let it be melted again over the fire. ¶ Secondly, dissolve the refined <note>original: *obliatum*, likely referring to the material being shaped into a small cake or wafer</note> material again fully upon a stone with "water of sal ammoniac" <term>sal armoniacus: ammonium chloride, used to dissolve metals</term>. Then coagulate it in a glass vessel closed at the top as I taught, and coagulate it with a very slow fire. Mix it well and coagulate it again with a very slow fire as before. Then increase the amount of the water of mercury and add as much refined and fixed "water of sulfur" as is made from the water of the moon. Mix it well and coagulate this whole mixture completely as described above. Then grind that coagulated matter very well upon a stone,

and imbibe it <note>to "imbibe" in alchemy is to slowly add liquid to a dry powder until it is saturated</note> while grinding with water of mercury. When it has been well processed, place it in strong glass "horns" <term>cornua vitrea: curved or horn-shaped glass vials</term> that are well-sealed with clay and closed tightly with a glowing hot iron as I taught above. Place these in warm horse dung <note>a standard alchemical method for providing gentle, constant heat</note> and turn it in water within 9 days until the mercury is fully fixed. Then distill it into water, throw away the dross <note>original: *feces*, the solid residue left after distillation</note>, and coagulate the distilled liquid. Then you will have a fixed and permanent tincture. Place 1 part of this tincture upon 100 parts of any purged and calcined metal; it is always good in every examination and will endure. Project <note>to "project" is to cast the alchemical medicine onto a molten metal</note> 1 part of this same substance upon 30 parts of purified crude mercury, and it will convert it into the purest silver <note>original: *luna*, the alchemical name for silver</note>.

¶ Secondly, grind the medicine upon a stone with the distilled water of fully refined mercury, and also place it in the glass horns in the dung just as before. Turn it as before, and once turned, distill and coagulate it as before. Then the tincture will be stronger and better. Place 1 part of this tincture upon 1,000 parts of any purged and calcined metal; it is always good and perfect in every examination. Project 1 part of the same upon 60 parts of purified crude mercury, and it will be converted into the purest silver.

### Here ends the Mirror of Lights.
### Thanks be to God.

There was a certain smith <note>original: "fabro," referring to a metalworker</note> in the land of the Ganori <note>the "Ganori" may refer to a specific ethnic group or a mythical location</note> staying in his chamber. A certain maimed man gave him two silver wheels <note>likely silver coins or discs</note> so that he might "sweat" them in the fire. When they were melted, the smith cast over them powders belonging to the Ganerus. It was a powder of a green color that produced a yellow smoke in the fire. The melted silver was immediately upgraded into gold. The smith then sold that gold to craftsmen at the price of the purest gold, and the craftsmen found it to be of the highest purity. The smith gave that payment to the Ganerus. At last, the Ganerus, moved by mercy and led by piety, revealed the composition of this powder, saying: "Take one part of pulverized gold and one part of burnt copper <term>aes ustum: copper oxidized by heat, often used as a coloring agent or catalyst</term>..."

# The History of Maurus.

Take one part of <term>Saffron of Iron<note>original: "croco ferri"; a red powder made of iron oxide, often used as a coloring agent or "tincture" in alchemy.</note></term> and a quantity of <term>Sal Ammoniac<note>original: "salis armoniaci"; ammonium chloride, a salt used to clean metals and act as a flux.</note></term> equal to it. Let the Sal Ammoniac be dissolved, and with it, let the powder be soaked through a long boiling. Afterward, let the whole be set to dissolve; when it has been dissolved, it is coagulated by the heat of hot ashes. One part of that coagulated substance <term>tinges<note>Stains or transforms the color and nature of the metal.</note></term> ten parts of silver into gold. And if it is dissolved and coagulated a second time, one part is sufficient for twenty; and if a third time, one part is sufficient for thirty, and so on, ascending in power.

Gold should be pulverized in this way: Let lead be melted in a vessel which has a small hole at the top, according to the quantity of gold. Let the gold be placed beneath it with a pair of tongs so that the gold, covering the hole, receives the smoke of the lead. When this is done several times, the gold will be able to be crushed into powder. 

<term>Saffron of Iron<note>original: "Crocus ferri".</note></term> is made thus: Let iron filings be touched by strong, clear red vinegar and placed in the hot sun for two or three days. Afterward, that vinegar is dried, and other vinegar is placed over the filings in the sun; this is done repeatedly until the entire mass of filings is dissolved and reduced into a very fine powder. Then, let all the vinegar evaporate from the filings in the sun. It should be known that before it is mixed with anything, it must be washed with pure water and with water and salt until the water comes away clear. I later saw that rare person alone who recounted these things, and I recounted the same

---

# The History of Maurus.

to a certain elderly relative of mine, and the old man worked with that same rare person in the same manner described above. However, without adding gold or silver, it took on the color of ancient gold. 

The solution of Sal Ammoniac is as follows: place it in a small vessel suspended in a pot having boiling water, and let it be covered quickly; for by the vapor of the water it will be dissolved. Distill it and reserve it. In this way, the solution of all salt, borax, and alum, etc., is made.

->Honor to God. A. M. G. H.<-

---

The <term>quaternary<note>The number four, representing the four physical elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note></term> being conquered by the <term>ternary<note>The number three, representing the spiritual trinity or the alchemical principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note></term>, the <term>septenary<note>The number seven, representing the seven planets/metals and spiritual perfection.</note></term> will rejoice in its exaltation.

### Cornelius Agrippa

> There is one thing created by God which is the subject of all wonderful art, which is in the earth and in the heavens. It is itself active as animal, vegetable, and mineral; found everywhere, known by very few, expressed by none by its own proper name, but veiled in innumerable figures and enigmas, without which neither Alchemy nor natural magic can reach their complete end.

It is redness in whiteness, just as <term>minium<note>Red lead.</note></term> is in <term>ceruse<note>White lead.</note></term>.

# There follows a French translation, somewhat more detailed, of the story of the Moor recounted at the end of the preceding Treatise.

<column-break/>

# Ancient History of silver transmuted into Gold by a Moor.

Take pulverized or calcined gold, brass <note>original: *airain*</note>, and Tincture <margin>Red tincture <note>original: ☉ rubra</note> from the fixed heights.</margin> Take equal parts of burnt gold and saffron of gold <term>saffron of gold: a name for a golden-colored powder, usually an oxide or chloride of gold</term>. Take an equal part of red-made sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used in alchemy to dissolve metals</term>. Others add an equal part of red-made vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate mineral used to create "acidic waters" for dissolving substances</term>. Then dissolve the Sal ammoniac, and once it is dissolved, "incerate" it—that is, soak the aforementioned powders of gold, brass, vitriol, and saffron of gold with this water, grinding them for a long time on a porphyry stone or marble. 

A long time after the powders have "drunk" all this salt water, place them in a long-necked glass vial in horse dung <note>Horse dung was a standard alchemical tool for providing a gentle, constant, and natural "digestive" heat</note> for the duration of 21 days <note>symbolized by the sign of Jupiter ♃</note>, changing the dung every 4 days. After it is well dissolved and turned into water, congeal <note>solidify</note> them in a vial, placing them in the furnace on hot ash. Of that congealed substance, place one part onto 10 parts of fine silver, which has been prepared and separated.

**Note.** If any part remains whole, it must be ground with the sal ammoniac water until everything is dissolved; do this for fear of losing your gold, always repeating the "imbibition" <note>soaking</note> with Sal ammoniac. If you resolve that congealment again, do everything in order as described... one part of it will tinge <note>The sentence ends here with the catchword "teindra" (will tinge), indicating the result of the transmutation continues on the next page.</note>

Fol. 33.

# The Work of Charles Haward
### Extracted from his own Testament.
### Year: 1611

### The Preparation of Cinnabar, from one pound of which the purest Quicksilver <term>☿: Mercury</term> and Gold <term>☉: Sol</term> are extracted.

Take <term>℞: recipe</term> 3 pounds of ordinary Quicksilver <term>☿: Mercury</term> and 1 pound of common sulfur. Melt the sulfur first in a copper mortar, and afterward cast in the Quicksilver. Stir it over the fire until the material begins to stick to the sides as if burnt. Then remove it and stir constantly until the Quicksilver disappears <note>The mercury is "killed" or stabilized by reacting with the sulfur to form a crude mercury sulfide</note>. Once finished, remove the material from the mortar, reduce it to a powder, and place it in a sublimating vessel. Seal it well with lute <note>lute: a clay or paste used to make a vessel airtight</note> and sublimate the material according to the art <note>original: "secundum artem," a standard phrase meaning to follow the proper chemical procedure</note>; this will be completed in the space of 24 hours.

Then take that sublimated Cinnabar and break it; that which is pulverized should be formed into small tablets with the white of eggs <note>original: "albumine Ouorum"</note> and dried. Then take 1 pound of our Blessed Salt <term>Sal Benedicti: a name often used for purified salt or a specific preparation of antimony</term> and 1 pound of Silver <term>☽: Luna</term> filings. Having mixed these together, you shall "cement" <note>cement: a process of heating a metal with other substances in layers</note> the Cinnabar in layers <note>original: "cementatōe SSS," meaning stratum super stratum or layer upon layer</note> in a well-luted vessel. Place this in the bottom of an ash-furnace, covered with ashes or sand to the thickness of two fingers. Then kindle the fire above and below for 10 hours of the "Mercury of the Sun" <note>original: "10 o ☿ ☉"</note>. First, for 26 hours <term>h: hours</term>, use a gentle fire, and then increase it by degrees until the end.

When these things are finished, test a small portion of the Cinnabar with a cupel <note>copella: a small, porous bowl used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals</note>, and you will find a proportion of Gold <term>☉: Sol</term>, or even more. Then take the Blessed Salt (or salt of antimony) and the Silver filings that were mixed in at first, and boil them in clear water until they dissolve. You will have your Silver cleaned and in the same quantity as before. Then filter that salt-water and congeal it, and you will have your Salt of Antimony back again. Its virtue and goodness remain the same as before, ready to perform the work again.

# Medicine for Projection
### Upon Copper <term>♀: Venus</term>, of which 1 ounce will give 1 ounce of Gold <term>☉: Sol</term>.

Take 2 pounds of our Antimony or Blessed Salt, and 1 pound of sublimated Sal Ammoniac. Infuse these in 4 pounds of strong spirit of vinegar. Leave it for 3 days <term>d: days</term> in a wide-bottomed glass vessel, stirring it at least ten times each day. Then pour off that spirit and filter it according to the art. Add 2 pounds of new spirit again, and leave it for 4 days, stirring it...

Cast it out, and you will find pure **Silver** <term>☽: the moon symbol, representing silver</term> of every proof.

# To make the Blessed Salt,
# or Salt of Antimony,
# proceed thus.

<margin>potentially <note>original: à posse</note></margin>
Take 1 pound of that pure **Antimony** <term>♁: a mineral used in alchemy, often associated with transformation</term> and reduce it to a very fine powder. Distribute this equally into 30 wide-bottomed glass vessels—namely, 4 pounds <note>The weights here appear contradictory in the original Latin (1 lb total vs 4 lbs per vessel); it may imply a larger scale or a specific ratio.</note> into each—over which you will pour two pints <term>sextarios: an ancient liquid measure</term> of noble spirit of vinegar. Leave it thus for 30 days, stirring it about ten times each day. 

Then, pour the liquid into other vessels and filter it; add new spirit of vinegar again, which you will leave for 4 or 5 days, mixing it as before ten times each day. By tilting the vessel, filter the liquid and discard the antimony, which is then of no use. Then, cause all that vinegar to evaporate in a water-bath <term>balneo: the 'bain-marie' or heated bath</term> according to the art. When the salt congeals on the surface, take it up with a wooden spoon. Afterward, dry it in the heat of the sun or by a slow fire <margin>* namely, do not use excessive heat lest the spirits evaporate.</margin>. 

In this operation, you must conduct yourself cautiously. By this means, you will have that Salt of Antimony required for the aforementioned work, which is more precious than gold <note>original: "auro contra non carum," an idiom meaning of immense value.</note>.

### For making the spirit of vinegar,
### serving the above-mentioned 
### work.

**Take** <note>original: "R", the recipe symbol for 'Recipe' or 'Take'</note> 12 sextarii <note>A Roman unit of volume; 12 sextarii is roughly equivalent to 1.5 gallons or 6 liters.</note> (or as much as you wish) of the most noble vinegar made from wine; and take care that you do not use any other than that made from wine, for vinegar made from other liquids—such as cider, beer, etc.—is entirely unsuitable for our work. Place that vinegar into a glass vessel, set it under an alembic <term>alembic: the upper part of a distilling apparatus</term>, and place it in a <term>B. M.</term> <note>original: "Balneum Mariae," or Mary’s Bath; a water bath used for gentle, uniform heating.</note>, first applying a gentle fire. When about a third part has distilled over, set that aside, for it is "phlegm" <note>In alchemy, phlegm refers to the watery, tasteless, and inactive portion of a distilled substance.</note> and not the spirit. Then, put a new receiving vessel in place, and you will have the remaining "best spirit" for the use mentioned above. The phlegm, however, is of no use except for common purposes and for cleaning ulcers by washing them with it.

**Basilius Valentinus** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, supposedly a Benedictine monk, whose writings were highly influential in the 17th century.</note> asserted that the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> is made from **Gold** <term>☉: the solar symbol for gold</term>; this is true indeed, but not without the assistance of natural salt, both simple and compound. For this reason, **Chrysippus Fanianus** <note>Johannes Chrysippus Fanianus, a 16th-century legal scholar and alchemical author.</note> not unsuitably named alchemy as if it were <term>Sal-chymia</term> <note>original: "Salt-chemistry."</note>. He said there is a twofold way of preparing it (namely, the most universal Stone): either by the composition of the Stone from its simple mineral root with dissolved **Gold** <term>☉</term>, or by the conjunction of **Gold** <term>☉</term> with compound mineral roots.

---

### Regarding the Blessed Salt,
### or Salt of Antimony, 
### he proceeds thus 
### in another Testament in its proper place.

This is the true matter from which you must extract the Spirit, which turns first into air and then into water, and is called the "Fire of the Philosophers," and Mercury, and Gold, and Silver. This spirit, having been rectified <note>Rectification is the process of purifying a liquid by repeated distillation.</note> seven times, dissolves **Gold** <term>☉</term>; afterwards, it is necessary to congeal and dissolve it again in the same water. Once you have this Blessed Salt, you need nothing else for making the medicine or Elixir, of whatever grade you wish to achieve.

**George Ripley** <note>An famous 15th-century English alchemist and canon of Bridlington, author of "The Compound of Alchymy."</note> treated the entire operation quite clearly, especially in his *Medulla* <note>original: "Medulla Alchimiae" or The Marrow of Alchemy, a popular 15th-century text.</note>.

Furthermore, know that after you have rectified the spirit seven times, you can perform a projection <note>The final act of alchemy: casting the medicinal powder onto base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note> at a ratio of 1 to 30 parts; but you must be certain of the quantity and quality for multiplication <note>The process of increasing the power or quantity of the alchemical agent.</note>. Dissolution, putrefaction, and congelation complete everything. Take care, however, that you do not use too much external fire, for our blessed spirit

♄ [Saturn/Lead] is cold and dry; therefore, it contains no more Mercury [☿] in itself than 3 ounces and 12 ounces of Jupiter [♃/Tin]. It is easily dissolved, and from it is drawn "living Mercury" and virgin's milk.

♃ [Jupiter/Tin] is hot and moist and good for animating our Moon-substance <gloss>lunaria</gloss>; it has in itself 3 ounces of Jupiter and 12 of Mercury.

♂ [Mars/Iron] is hot and dry; therefore, it has no more than 2 ounces of Mercury and 14 of Jupiter.

☉ [The Sun/Gold] is the most temperate of all the Planets, and its complexion is an equality and clarity of quality, etc., and in it are the four elements. For they are complete, and from it, one has the elixir of the great Sun [Gold], hot and moist, the king of 24 parts Jupiter, and 15 <note>original: "i v", likely 15</note> Mercury.

♀ [Venus/Copper] is of a hot and dry nature, yet less so than Mars, and with her, our Moon-substance <gloss>lunaria</gloss> is improved; she is pure in her antiquity.

☿ [Mercury/Quicksilver] is of equal proportion, and contains 8 parts of Saturn and Jupiter.

☽ [The Moon/Silver] is our cold and moist substance, and from it is made the tincture for the white <note>An alchemical preparation intended to transmute base metals into silver.</note>; it contains in itself pure and shining Mercury and has 2 parts Jupiter and 14 parts Saturn.

Section 1. concerning the nature and manner of getting the matter.

Vincentius <note>Likely Vincent of Beauvais, a medieval scholar whose works were often cited by alchemists.</note> says: The works of God took their beginning from Light, by which <gloss>which</gloss> example he has taught man to work upon light. And Raymond Lully <note>A famous 13th-century philosopher and purported alchemist.</note> says: My son, when thou wilt begin to work our Mastery, beware thou work not but of the Nature and matter of light, and not of other things, of which the lower world is created.

Morien <note>Morienus, a legendary hermit-alchemist of the 7th century.</note>, concerning the contents and resemblance of the matter, says: in the stone are the 4 Elements contained, and it is likened to the world, that can be likened to this, to wit in Creation or Nature. Whosoever therefore...

<meta>This page transitions from the planetary calculations of the previous section to the philosophical core of the "Great Work." Therefore, whoever shall seek any other stone in this Mastery, his work shall be altogether frustrated.

By these things aforesaid, all animals, vegetables, and minerals, and whatever can be gotten from any of them, are utterly excluded. First, because they are from the lower world and its matter. Second, because no specific creature is produced according to the pattern or method of the formation and composition of the world; which was created into Heaven <margin>* Genesis 1.</margin> and earth <insert>*</insert> from a dark and watery substance immediately created from pure and sincere light—namely, from God the Best <note>original: "D. O.", likely *Deo Optimo*, a common abbreviation for God the Best and Greatest</note>—which is the true pattern proposed by the philosophers for imitation, in all resemblance from and forth of the next Essence to that supreme Light.

### §. 2. Concerning the Monad, or the Unity of Matter.

<margin>Agadmon in the *Assembly of Philosophers*, page 20. <note>original: "Turba", referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note></margin> Leave behind all plurality, for Nature is indeed content with One; he who is ignorant of this cannot help but go astray in the Mastery.

<margin>Morienus. <note>A legendary 7th-century alchemist and hermit.</note></margin> The first and principal Matter and Substance of this Thing is one, and from it, one Thing proceeds, and with it it is made, nor is anything added to it or diminished from it; Wherefore <margin>Fol. 3.</margin> <margin>* Pontanus <note>Johann Pontanus, a famous alchemist often cited for his "secret fire."</note> says:</margin> whoever separates anything from the subject knows nothing at all in our Philosophy. And all Philosophers agree that their Mastery <term>Magisterium</term> <note>The complete process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is the work of Nature, and not of the Artisan. <margin>** For whenever the Philosophers speak of actions or operations regarding the Mastery, it ought to be understood as being about the operations of Nature, in which the mystery of the Philosophers lies hidden.</margin> <margin>w * *The Rosary*, folio 143. ** *The Rosary* to Bishop Sarasantus.</margin> Again, in our water and through it, all kinds of operations <margin>q. T. vol. 2. p. 163.</margin>—namely Sublimations, Distillations, solutions, calcinations, fixations, and the rest—are perfected, as if in an artificial vessel, which is the greatest secret. And all those of the *Assembly* <note>original: "Turba"</note> unanimously affirm that our stone <margin>Artephius, Zacharias.</margin> dissolves itself, kills itself, and brings itself back to life, coagulates, turns black, washes, whitens, and turns red. Thus, there is no manual work to be prepared or perfected by way of separation, distillation, purification, or conjunction, as the common sort of artists presume.

**Hercules the Philosopher.** This Mastery proceeds first of all from one root, which is afterwards divided into many things, and returns again into one.

**Hermes.** He commanded that all things proceed from one. <note>A reference to Hermes Trismegistus and the *Emerald Tablet*.</note>

<summary>This page establishes the fundamental alchemical principle that the "Stone" must come from a single, unified source rather than a mixture of substances. It argues that the alchemical process is a natural cycle of death and rebirth that occurs within a single "water," rather than through the manual labor of a chemist.</summary>

Fol. 38.

they proceed; in this way my mastery <note>term: magisterium; the completion of the Great Work or the philosopher's stone itself</note> is made from one substance.

**Martinus.** That Master has proceeded well who began in one, and ends in the same.

**Ripley & The Ladder of Philosophers.** The parts of our most precious stone’s matter are co-essential <note>sharing the same fundamental nature</note> and joined together, and there is no true beginning except for one.

**Alphidius.** When Nature disposes a certain matter, which contains within itself all necessary things, all operations are perfected from it and through it.

**Valerandus Syluensis.** True Nature has prepared the matter required for our work, and has handed it over in a certain specification and manner which is known to Philosophers alone; nor does it need any other artificial transformation outside of itself.

Many others write in the same sense, but through these authors it can be clear enough that after Nature has supplied the matter, all manual operations ought to cease. The first root is only one single thing, without any artificial addition, separation, or reduction.

### §. 3. What this matter must contain, to fulfill the various opinions of the Philosophers.

Since various Philosophers write about White water, golden water, vitriol water, and the antimonial Saturnine Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum vivo"</note>—names which suggest the red, the black, and the white stages; and likewise I speak of the white man, the red man, and his white wife, which are those two Mercurial seeds, called by some the **Green Lion** because of their rawness, or immaturity so to speak, in that state; likewise they write about perfect and imperfect bodies, and about metals.

<margin>The Rosary, 
Artephius, 
Trevisan, 
etc.</margin>
Although no manual operation is to be employed in the Philosophical work, because (as all affirm) it is not a manual work, nor is it to be forced by any art (as some madmen assert), but is instead made by the commerce of nature through nature itself, and by its service alone. Nevertheless, whoever—in order to confirm the truth of that matter which must be treated by Nature itself—cannot find that middle nature, the white soul, and at that very same time, the black and the red, the vitriolic and golden water flowing through the alembic <note>a vessel used for distillation</note> in small droplets, partly white and partly red (which the Philosophers call the Red Living One and the White Woman), and the rusty substance.

### §. 4. On the vessel.

By some it is called the Athanor <note>term: Athanor; a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant temperature for long periods</note>, the furnace of the Philosophers, or the triple vessel, the description of which is found in Trevisanus, Flamel, and others. 
Ripley says: One thing, one glass, one furnace, and no more. And I would dare to affirm confidently that whoever requires more besides that one Thing, one glass, and one furnace, shall never reach the perfection of the Philosophical work. Our Mercury, says Trevisanus, must cook itself <note>original: "concoquere"; a process of heating or "digesting" the matter</note> in a triple vessel, and this is to evaporate and convert the activity and dryness of the Fire into the vaporous humidity of the air surrounding the matter.

### §. 5. On the fire and its Governance.

Always keep in mind to maintain a uniform fire <margin>Pandulphus, Theatrum Chemicum page 112.</margin> or one of the same intensity, from the beginning to the end.
Rosinus to Sarat <note>referring to a dialogue in the alchemical compendium "Theatrum Chemicum"</note>, page 184: You must complete the work through the action of the heat of a certain fire, which does not sublimate <note>term: sublimation; the process where a solid turns into gas without becoming liquid; here, the heat must be gentle enough not to force the matter upward too violently</note> because it is so gentle,

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the alchemical process, moving from the description of the vessel and the matter into the specific conditions of the first stage of the Work, "putrefaction," and the fundamental governing principles of the Art.</meta>

Fol. 40.

**The Rosary of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Rosarius Philosop."; a seminal 16th-century alchemical text</note>, page 169. Let **putrefaction** <note>the stage of decay or blackening, essential for the rebirth of the matter</note> be performed in the gentlest fire of warm and moist dung, and in no other, so that nothing may ascend; for if anything should ascend, a separation of parts would occur there, which must not happen until the male and female <note>referring to the active and passive principles, typically Sulfur and Mercury</note> perfectly unite, and one receives the other—the sign of which is recognized by the skill or nature of a perfect **dissolution**.

---

Note: The efficacy of the whole art consists in two things: **Dissolve and Coagulate** <note>original: "Solve & coagula"; the central maxim of alchemy, referring to breaking down substances and rebuilding them</note>. Dissolution is twofold; the first dissolves bodies into mercury—this is done through mercury and regards particular things; the other is truly the "Great Mercurial" [solution]. This belongs to the universal work and is a certain transmutation of metallic bodies into their own **prime matter** <note>original: "primam materiam"; the original, formless substance from which all things are made</note>. Although certain learned men think it impossible for the spirits of minerals to be fixed with a few bodies, the contrary is clear from the authorities of many masters of this art.

**Geber** <note>the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Arabic alchemy</note>: Chapter 32 of *The Perfect Mastery*. Spirits are similarly mixed with **Gold** itself and are fixed through it by great skill, which is not achieved by a "stiff-necked" <note>stubborn or inflexible</note> craftsman.

And the **Ladder of the Philosophers** <note>original: "scala Philosop."; likely referencing the *Scala Philosophorum*, an anonymous alchemical treatise</note> says: spirits are congealed by the benefit of dissolution, for the fixed [parts] of both the body and that mixture with **quicksilver** <note>original: "argento vivo"; mercury</note>.

**Quercetanus** <note>Joseph Duchesne, a French physician and influential Paracelsian alchemist</note> in his *Tetrad*, acknowledges that the power of extracting metals is contained in the spirits of **sea salt**.

Salt is, for **Paracelsus** <note>the revolutionary 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist</note>, the "lesser circulation" <note>original: "circulatum minus"; a specialized solvent prepared from salt</note>, which he calls the matrix of all metals by its own right—especially sea salt.

## Extracts from the Manuscripts of the aforementioned Charles Haward.

### For Gold <note>original: ☉, the symbol for the Sun and Gold</note>

Take thin plates of calcined copper <note>original: ♀, the symbol for Venus and Copper</note> which have been reduced again into a body, and smear them very well with honey. Then take Alexandrian Tutty <term>Tutty: a crude zinc oxide used in making brass</term> (which should be white on one side and lemon-yellow on the other); reduce it to a powder, and you shall cover the plates on all sides by spreading it over them. First [smear] the bottom of the crucible <gloss>pot</gloss> <note>original: 🜔</note> with honey, and above that a little of the powder of <unclear>strica</unclear> also, until it is covered. Then grind raisins and currants into small pieces, and from them make little cakes equal in size to the plates. Thus you shall place the plates of the aforementioned copper into the crucible <note>original: 🜔</note>, interposing a little cake between each, and so [layer them] until the crucible <gloss>pot</gloss> <note>original: 🜔</note> is filled. When this is done, seal it with lute <term>lute: a clay-based cement used to airtight-seal alchemical vessels</term> and close it exactly according to the art. Then apply a violent fire for 14 hours, and you will have a most beautiful metal, of which you will take the following proportion: namely, 6 parts of copper against 3 parts of gold and 1 part of the purest silver <note>original: ☽, the symbol for the Moon</note>. Melt those thus mixed together, and throw a little saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note> on top. <insert>*</insert> Then, when it has run for some space of time, throw it into the powder [within the] <insert>crucible</insert>, and let it cool there; then take the metal again.

<margin>
<insert>*</insert> The original English manuscript has: "and when it hath drive well."
</margin>

<column-break/>

[Take the] metal and melt it again, and throw it into green copper <note>likely verdigris or a copper-based solution</note>, and thus melt and throw it into those powders alternately 3 or 4 times, and you will have pure gold. If indeed to the proportions previously prescribed you add as much weight again of copper and silver, you will have gold which will equal the "touch" <note>the touchstone test for purity</note> of 24 carats; but nevertheless, it will fail somewhat in weight.

## If you wish to exalt the color of the Metal, the following cement <term>cement: a dry corrosive powder used to refine or color metals by layering them without melting</term>

Grind the moist ingredients together, and soak them in ox spleen; heat it at the fire, and with these things cement the aforementioned metal without melting. An experiment of this process was made in the year 1592, as my author reports.

# Treatise on the Stone of the Philosophers. From the same Manuscript of Haward.

Pliny the Philosopher says: "Beloved son, if you desire to find our stone, seek it above all things in the dung <note>original Latin: *tino*, likely a variation of *fimo*, meaning dung or waste</note>, and if it happens that you do not find it there, keep your hand away from your purse <note>original: *manum a crumena abstine*; a warning not to waste money on expensive ingredients if the basic matter is not understood</note>." All things that are of great price in this art are but feigned and unprofitable; for even the poor possess this stone aforesaid, for truly it is thrown in the high ways. And because of the diversity of colors, Philosophers have called it by various names; and as the colors are varied and changed, so they give to it different names, and speak darkly in their sayings, so that none but their 'children' should understand the truth. And they call their 'children' those who perfectly understand their writings—and there are but few that understand them truly, etc.

Our stone is a <unclear>thing</unclear> drawn and separated from our body, which is called the <term>first matter</term> <note>The *prima materia*, the primitive, formless base of all matter</note>. Therefore, according to the Philosophers, the separation of it is a division into four.

<column-break/>

Into four elements. Take <note>original: *R.*, the standard abbreviation for *Recipe*</note> first: that the body be dissolved into the first matter; also the first matter must be separated into its four elements, so that each element is reduced into the nature of elements by <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The process of rotting or breaking down the substance to its base state</note>. For corruption is the principle of generation in both natural and artificial things. And I say unto you, trust that the kind of <term>Quick-silver</term> <note>original: *Argent vive*</note>—which is called quick <margin>* the observation is in the original MS.</margin> * silver—shall not be changed into the kind of gold <note>original: *aurum*</note> <insert>za <unclear>lum</unclear> is</insert> which is called * unconquered <note>original: *invictus*</note>, nor vice versa <note>original: *e converso*</note>.

For in very truth, the stone is not made nor is it gathered from diverse things, as many fools suppose and believe, but it is made only of one thing and only of one body. It is combined and made, and it has one name, and that name is given to it; and it is named or called vulgarly and commonly by every man, which no man truly knows, however that may be. Every man has it and no man has it; every man knows it—this is true, for to him to whom it is shown, he knows it immediately. Moreover, each man has it—and this is true, for each man may have it easily. No man has it—and this is true, for although you have the stone...

<summary>This page introduces a theoretical treatise on the Philosophers' Stone, emphasizing that the "first matter" is a common, inexpensive substance often overlooked by the ignorant. It describes the necessity of breaking the matter down into four elements through putrefaction before it can be transformed.</summary>

### Process.

Take, in the name of the blessed Trinity, our body incorruptible <term>Adrope</term>, and beat that into thin plates, and after put them into a glass vessel, and add to that body the corrosive matter, and close well the mouth of the vessel with the "Lute of the Philosophers" <note>A specialized alchemical sealant used to make vessels airtight</note>, which you shall make of Bole Armoniac <note>A medicinal red clay used in laboratory seals</note>.

### If you wish to make the Red elixir,

### A recipe that G. R., Canon of Bridlington <note>Bridlington Priory in Yorkshire was a center of learning; a "Canon" was a member of the religious community there. This likely refers to George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist.</note>, sent to his friend, which he himself had proved to be true.

And first of gold <note>symbol: ☉</note> which I think is so, if he had as plainly discovered the salt that will not cost money <note>original: "my d."; 'd' stands for denarius or pence, a common abbreviation for money</note> as he has done the rest of the process.

**Take** <note>original: ℞, the traditional symbol for a recipe or "take thou"</note> the weight of fine gold <note>symbol: ☉</note> of the old Edward noble <note>A high-purity gold coin first minted during the reign of Edward III</note>, and dissolve <note>original: "repel", used here in the sense of breaking the metal's solid form</note> it with five <note>original: "v"</note> times as much fine mercury <note>original symbol: ☿; the OCR "v" likely misread the mercury symbol or refers to "five" in this instance</note>, well scoured and cleansed. And you must continue in the grinding and fretting them upon a marble stone with strong <unclear>urine</unclear> <note>OCR: "scating run"; stale or "strong" urine was frequently used by early chemists for its ammonia content</note> and salt, every now and then washing the amalgam <note>A mixture of mercury with another metal, in this case, gold</note> in hot strong <unclear>urine</unclear> until the salt be clean released <note>original: "relente"</note> from the amalgam. 

Then put it in a glass with a long neck in a round case of lead that will stand in a brass pot's bottom; but see the mouth of the glass be closely stopped. And put water into the brass pot and cause it to boil about the glass as a <term>Water Bath</term> <note>original: "Balneo", referring to a *Balneum Mariae* or Marie's Bath; a vessel of water used to heat another container gently and evenly</note> for the space of a day and a night. Then take forth the glass with the amalgam, and strain it through a linen cloth doubled, with some part of the mercury <note>symbol: ☿</note> that is dissolved into it, which will go through the cloth.

<summary>This page provides a practical alchemical recipe for creating a gold-mercury amalgam, attributed to the famous Canon of Bridlington, George Ripley. It details the cleaning of gold coins and the use of a water bath (balneum) for heating the mixture.</summary>
<keywords>Gold, Mercury, Amalgam, George Ripley, Bridlington, Alchemical Recipe, Edward Noble, Balneum, Laboratory Practice</keywords>

...and cloth in Mercury <note>original symbol: ☿</note>. Keep that by itself and add to it other essence <term>Eny</term> of Gold <note>original symbol: ☉</note>. So the part remaining undissolved in the cloth—and do therein in every way as you did before, so continuing the work of solution until all the Gold is strained through the cloth and none remains. Once that is done, set all the dissolved matter together for 3 days and 3 nights in a water bath <note>original: 'B.' for Balneum Mariae, a vessel of warm water or sand used for gentle heating</note> until it is better dissolved into its elements. When that is done, strain it often through a linen cloth until it is perfectly dried, and then put it in a long-necked flask <term>bolte head</term>—the longer the better—and set it on a furnace. Continue the work of precipitation until it turns into a red powder like sand. Which work if you do not know, I shall teach it to you; for in that point, as far as I could ever perceive, is the whole effect and end of the Philosophers <term>Phis</term>, who teach the methods of fire and of their skill. If you cannot do it, then read the Philosophers' works and understand them correctly, which are these:

taken here as that time of the year when the Sun is in its highest house and in its highest degree, and so must your fire pass the test of experience. Then, when you have reduced the matter into a red powder, continue the work for a day and a night in the same heat equally. Lastly, take it out of your glass and grind it on a marble stone with salt <note>original symbol: 🜔</note> that has been very strongly prepared into a dissolved matter. (I do not write down how to do that, but I shall tell you, and understand that the aforementioned skillful salt profits the work well. <note>The scribe writes 'Antition', possibly meaning 'Addition' or 'Attrition'.</note> It does not cost you much.) Let there be as much of this salt as there is powder, and let it dissolve for the space of a lunar month <note>original symbol: ☽</note>. Then put it in a water bath until it is clearly dissolved, as clear as fine wine. Then, in a long receiving vessel, precipitate it with the final heat for the space of a week; let it vaporize and congeal, etc., and you shall have a truly precious ruby stone. Frequent dissolving and congealing makes the Elixir <term>Elixir</term> more rich and strong. Cast it upon Mercury and in the process of alloying <term>amaling</term>, etc.

Take one pound of Antimony <note>original symbol: ♋, referring to the mineral stibnite or its refined metallic form</note> and put it in a receiver. Add your medicinal powder to it and work it for a day and a night after the manner of precipitation, and it will be fixed into a powder. One ounce <note>original: ℥j</note> of this powder should be used upon one pound of fine Silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note>, or upon Mercury <note>original symbol: ☿</note> prepared with Antimony, or upon two ounces of Antimony <note>original symbol: ♁, occasionally used for 'Earth' or Antimony</note> or three ounces of Copper <note>original symbol: ♀</note>. Let it be reduced to a powder with soft fire <note>original symbol: Δ</note>, and then blow upon it with a great fire. Let it remain perfectly clear upon the cupel <note>original: "test"</note> like water <note>original symbol: ≈</note>, and then let it stand clear with the water until it is cold. When that is done, take out the mass of fine Gold <note>original symbol: ☉</note>.

I ask that you take this thankfully; for if you find a book or a man to teach you this, our medicine, while you live, know <note>original: "ke."</note> that as God shall save me, it would be to me the greatest displeasure that you can [give me if you reveal it]. And if you perceive [the secret], pray for me that I may come to the end of my life’s term, and I shall never rest until I come to the place where Jesus was, between the living <note>original: "quick"</note> and the dead. And if it please Him, I may live to perform it, etc.

<note>The scribe or a later reader adds: "It appears by this that he was going to Jerusalem on Pilgrimage."</note>

## To fix an Amalgam.

Take <note>original: $R_x$</note> one pound of it and distill all the moisture from it by dry fire <note>original symbol: Δ</note>. Then put it in a broad vessel and set it on the fire with a paper dipped in vinegar <note>original symbol: ♄, likely used here for Acetum</note>; it will burn like *aqua vitae* <note>Latin: "water of life," referring to concentrated flammable alcohol</note> until it is dry and calcined. Then take vinegar and add it to the calcined matter and let it boil to dissolve. Draw off that which is dissolved, and so dissolve it all; then distill the vinegar from it and it will become a red oil, called Incombustible Oil <note>original: Oleum incombustibile</note>, which will fix any amalgam <note>original symbol: 🜎</note>.

## Work for Silver according to Rhasis.
<note>original: "opus ad ☽ secundu Rhasis"; Rhasis refers to the 9th-century Persian alchemist Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi, whose works were foundational to European alchemy.</note>

Take <note>original: $R_x$</note> thin plates of Silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note> and five pounds of arsenic with three ounces <note>original: ℥iij</note> of salt and one ounce of steel filings. Grind all three of these together strongly. Afterwards, take the powder and put a portion of it in the bottom of a pot, and put a thin broad plate of silver upon it. Then cover it again with the powder, and then put another plate, and so cover or bury all the plates in the said powder. Afterward, make an easy fire of coals, and put into the vessel the powder of the strongest vinegar <note>original symbol: ♄</note> to moisten the powder with, and then set it upon the coals from the morning until it is three or four o'clock in the afternoon. Afterward, let it stand to dry strongly. Then, when it has so stood and is sufficiently dry, seal it <term>Lute</term> <note>to close a vessel airtight using a paste of clay or lime</note> fast and surely and bury it in coals. Then kindle the fire and make a strong fire for the space of twelve hours afterward.

<summary>This page details several alchemical recipes for "fixing" metals and creating "Incombustible Oil," alongside a personal note revealing the author's intent to go on a pilgrimage to Jerusalem. It includes a specific procedure for silver attributed to the famous Persian alchemist Rhasis (Al-Razi).</summary>

After, remove the Fire <note>original symbol: 🜂</note> and let it cool; and when it is cold, open the pot. And thou shalt find the plates mixed with the powder. The part thereof of Elixir [put] upon 20 [parts] of Copper <note>original symbol: ♀</note> and it shall be converted into Silver <note>original symbol: ☽</note> without reproach.

Therefore take and lute it, and congeale it in a pot with ashes, and after cast thereof Elixir upon 250 [parts] of Copper <note>original symbol: ♀</note>, and thou shalt see a wonder of Philosophy.

<meta>Folio 50 follows an unnumbered verso page. This page continues the collection of alchemical recipes and experimental notes, focusing on the transmutation and "multiplication" of metals, specifically silver and tin, using mercurial solvents.</meta>

Fol. 50.

Brimstone <term>unslaked</term> <note>original: "unslen"; refers to raw or untreated sulfur</note> is melted in the alabaster pot, and so let it continue three days and three nights in the boiling brimstone. # A work for the multiplication of Silver. By Fr. G. TS.
<note>original: "opus ad multipl. ☽"</note>

**Solvent.** <note>original: "Menstruum"</note> Take 2 pounds of <term>Roman Vitriol</term> <note>copper sulfate</note>, 1 pound of <term>Niter</term> <note>saltpeter</note>, and 3 ounces of <term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note>. Grind them upon <term>porphyry</term> <note>a hard stone used for grinding</note>, then pour them into a well-sealed <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel</note> and apply graduated <term>fire</term> <note>symbol: △</note>. When the neck of the cucurbit appears white from the heat, leave the Mercury there until it cools. Then place the liquid in the <term>alembic</term> <note>distillation head</note> with 3 ounces of powdered mercury. Then, as before, use liquid and fire, and the second time urge the fire violently until it is ignited in the <term>cupel</term> <note>a shallow porous dish used in refining</note>, which you shall then harden.

<margin>See the work of the Italian Marco Antonio. Manuscript page 24, where the extraction of the spirit is established according to the philosophical rule. Also Codex D in quarto, Folio 13. Others from Codex E, page 41 and following.</margin>
**Extraction of Mercury from Silver.** <note>original: "Extr. ☿. ex ☽."</note> Take as much very pure Silver as you wish. Dissolve it in common <term>water</term> <note>symbol: ▽; likely refers to a corrosive acid here</note>, grind it upon <term>porphyry</term>, and soak it with <term>oil of mercury</term>. Then apply <term>fire</term> <note>symbol: △</note> and repeat this operation three times. Afterwards, place the <term>impalpable mercury</term> <note>ground so fine it cannot be felt</note> which is like oil into a well-sealed glass <term>alembic</term>. Apply <term>fire</term> until the Silver is converted into Mercury. Then you shall grind and strain it.

**The multiplication of the Mercury of Silver with common mercury into Tin.** <note>original: "multipl. ☿. ☽. cum vulg. ad ♃." ♃ is the symbol for Jupiter, representing Tin.</note>
Take 2 ounces of Silver, well-filed. Make an amalgam with 6 ounces of well-washed common mercury. Place 1 ounce of the Silver into a long-necked <term>matrass</term> <note>a round-bottomed flask</note> and light it in the first degree of heat until the mass turns black. Then increase to the second degree until it glows. Then a third degree until it turns white. Thus you have all the colors. It is a most perfect Tin.

**Multiplication for lower Tin.** <note>original: "multipl. ad inf. ♃."</note> Take 1 ounce and place it in a matrass; pour over it half an ounce of common mercury. Then seal it with <term>lute</term> <note>clay or paste used to seal vessels</note> and place it in the <term>distillation vessel</term> <note>symbol: ⚗</note>. Light the continuous fire in the 1st, 2nd, and 3rd degrees until the mass turns white, and so proceed until it is sufficient.

**The conversion of Tin into the body of Silver.** <note>original: "conuersio ♃ in corpus ☽."</note> Take 1 ounce of very pure Tin. Dissolve it in <term>fire</term>. Then, into the dissolved mass, throw in 2 ounces of warm globules <note>likely of mercury or silver</note> until you have added 3 ounces of Tin, which will appear as the purest Silver to every test.

---

# A double work for both.
<note>Likely referring to the "White Work" for silver and the "Red Work" for gold.</note>

->Translated from the German Manuscripts<-
->of Mr. Rumler and transcribed from the book<-
->titled, **The Lily Among Thorns**<-
<note>original: "Lilium inter Spinas"</note>

Take pure quantities of <term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note> as you know. Grind them very small upon a stone until they become impalpable. Place them in a glass dish with the lid closed; put it in the digestion furnace for 12 <term>days</term> <note>symbol: ☉ (Sun)</note> for digesting and <term>calcining</term> <note>heating to a powder</note>. For the longer it is calcined, the more quickly it is dissolved in a hot and humid place with the Mercury. When it has been thus prepared and calcined, <insert>remove it and place it in a <term>Bain-Marie</term> <note>a water bath</note> so that</insert> it floats in the hot vapors over the water of the Bain-Marie. Let it remain there until the Mercury becomes entirely white and is thus dissolved into a liquid Mercury. This happens because of its good calcination and grinding, provided the hot and humid vapors do not touch the mass in the glass. When it has been dissolved through the Bain-Marie, you will have the most beautiful **virgin's milk** <note>original: "lac virginis," a term for a white alchemical liquid or suspension</note>, bitter-sour...

Fol. 51.

...of a bitter-acid taste; if 1 drop is dripped upon glowing **Gold** <note>symbol: ☉; original: "☉ candente"</note>, you whiten it from the inside and out <term>extra</term> <note>originally: "extra", here used to mean the external surface</note>.
<margin>You whiten with Virgin's Milk. Water, the 1st element.</margin>
This is called **Virgin’s Milk** <note>A common alchemical term for a white, milky liquid, often a mercurial solvent or a precipitate</note>, the 1st element, namely **Water** <note>symbol: ∇</note>. From the **Caput Mortuum** <note>literally "dead head"; the solid residue remaining after distillation</note> are extracted the two remaining elements: **Fire** <note>symbol: △</note> and **Air** <note>symbol: 🜁; transcribed as "A"</note>. And there will remain in the bottom the **Earth** <note>symbol: 🜃; transcribed as "□"</note>.

<margin>Separation of the elements.</margin>
Having now obtained the 1st element, **Water** <note>symbol: ∇</note>, grind the remaining material finely upon a stone. Place it in a glass dish as mentioned above and put it into digestion for 8 days or longer, and let it be well <term>calcined</term> <note>burned to a powder to remove volatile impurities</note>. Afterward, take it out and grind it again. Place it in a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel</note> with a <term>blind alembic</term> <note>a distillation lid without a spout, used to force vapors to condense and fall back into the vessel</note> in a **Water Bath** <note>Latin: Balneum Mariae; a vessel of hot water used for gentle, even heating</note> until <term>distilled</term> <note>symbol: ~</note>. If you can be patient and long-suffering, it will dissolve into itself without the addition of the 1st element, **Water** <note>symbol: ∇</note>. But if it should stand too long, pour over it the 1st element, **Water** <note>symbol: ∇</note>. Seal it carefully, place it in the Water Bath, and let them be well mixed and incorporated for 7 days. Then take the material and place it in a **Fire** <note>symbol: △</note> strong enough to stand, with even stronger **Air** <note>symbol: 🜁</note> than the Bath. Thus will ascend a beautiful oil of a golden color; and when it no longer wishes to stand, you will have in the receiver two elements: **Air** <note>symbol: 🜁</note> and **Fire** <note>symbol: △</note>. Then place it in the Water Bath and let it stand, and the **Fire** <note>symbol: △</note> will remain, which you shall keep by itself. But take the **Caput Mortuum** <note>the residue</note> in the Bath...

<column-break/>

...the residue from the distillation, which you should grind finely and place in a covered dish in the digestion furnace to be calcined for several days. Then take it out and grind it finely, and pour over it 4 parts of the **Virgin’s Milk** water. Once well mixed, place it in a <term>blind circulatory vessel</term> <note>symbol: 🜫; a vessel for long-term heating where vapors cycle</note> for 7 days. Then, having placed an <term>alembic</term> <note>distillation head</note> on top, let it be put into the furnace to withstand a strong **Fire** <note>symbol: △</note>, driving it as long as anything will ascend. Afterward, place that which is in the receiver into the Water Bath and let the Virgin's Milk stand, and there will remain a red oil, or **Air** <note>symbol: 🜁</note>, which is the third element. But the 4th element,
<margin>Red oil. Air, the 3rd element.</margin>
namely **Earth** <note>symbol: 🜃</note>, remains in the bottom, black like coals. This **Earth** <note>symbol: 🜃</note> you shall whiten in the following way.
<margin>Earth. Black. 4th element.</margin>

**Take** <note>symbol: ℞; transcribed as 🜔</note> the black **Earth** <note>symbol: 🜃</note> and grind it finely upon a stone and place it in a strong, luted <note>sealed with "lute," a clay-like paste</note> vessel. Put it in a **reverberatory furnace** <note>a furnace that reflects heat back onto the substance</note> to be calcined, and let it be calcined for many weeks, and it will become white. When it is already white, take it out and grind it finely upon a stone and <term>imbibe</term> <note>soak or saturate</note> it with its own **Water** <note>symbol: ∇</note>, namely the Virgin's Milk, as much as it weighs or a little less, so that it becomes like...
<margin>Joining of the elements and digestion for the Stone.</margin>

<summary>This page describes the separation of the four alchemical elements—Water (Virgin's Milk), Fire, Air (Gold/Red Oil), and Earth—from a base substance. # Another Beautiful Work.

<margin>Medicine for the white [work] or for projection upon <term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note>.</margin>
<margin>Its exaltation to the Red.</margin>
<term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note> is one Dragon which devours another, of which it has already been spoken. Take 2 parts each of <term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note>, <term>Salt</term> <note>symbol: 🜔</note>, and <term>Water</term> <note>symbol: 🜄</note>. Let the <term>Mercury</term> stand for a space of time <note>original: "per sps," likely abbreviation for "per spatium"</note> (until it is nearly diminished), then to the lower part of the <term>cucurbit</term> add 1 <term>Loth</term> <note>original: "lotonem"; a historical unit of weight, roughly 10-15 grams</note> of <term>Mercury</term> and pour the <term>Salt</term> <note>symbol: 🜔</note> and <term>Water</term> <note>symbol: 🜄</note> from above. Take care that it is not mixed, and that what is below comes to the top (otherwise it would be in vain). Seal the <term>cucurbit</term> <term>hermetically</term> <note>meaning to seal a glass vessel by melting the neck shut</note>, and place over it a cap with <term>Water</term> <note>symbol: 🜄</note>. Let the <term>Fire</term> <note>symbol: 🜂</note> be kindled, first slow, then increased to the third degree. And let it be left in that heat regime for 12 weeks. If all the <term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note> has turned into a red <term>Antimony</term> <note>symbol: ⚩</note> <term>Salt</term> <note>symbol: 🜔</note> precipitate, then let it be cooled. Take a new <term>cucurbit</term> and put in 1 part of crude <term>Mercury</term> <note>symbol: ☿</note>, and over this 2 parts of red <term>Antimony</term> <note>symbol: ⚩</note> <term>Salt</term> <note>symbol: 🜔</note>. Place it in hot <term>ashes</term> <note>symbol: 🜊; a sand or ash bath used for even, indirect heating</note> for 12 weeks, and again it will become red <term>Antimony</term> <note>symbol: ⚩</note>, which you grind again, and put 2 parts to 1 part
<margin>*originally <term>Salt</term> <note>symbol: 🜔</note></margin>
and again let it be left for 12 weeks in hot <term>ashes</term> <note>symbol: 🜊</note>. These things having been done, the red <term>Antimony</term> <note>symbol: ⚩</note> shall be called the <term>Essence</term> <note>symbol: ℰ</note> of <term>Gold</term> <note>symbol: ☉</note>, which <term>Essence</term> you shall take out from the glass and, after it is finely ground, you shall place it in a strong glass <term>cucurbit</term>.
